<DIV style="font-family: 'Times New Roman',Times,serif">

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The United States Patent and Trademark Office has issued United States Patents 6,129,930,
6,080,428, 6,406,715, 6,676,967, 6,746,691 and 6,818,229 to the Company with claims relating to,
among other things, <I>Niaspan&#146;s </I>and <I>Advicor&#146;s </I>formulation and method-of-use consistent with the
recommended once-a-day dosing regimen. On February&nbsp;7, 1997, Kos entered into a cross-licensing
agreement with a generic manufacturer. The Company subsequently purchased the two patents that were
the subject of such original agreement and agreed to continue paying a royalty to the generic
manufacturer on terms similar to those contained in the original agreement.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B><I>Cardizem LA </I></B><B>Litigation</B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;On July&nbsp;7, 2005, the Company received notice from Biovail that Andrx had filed with the FDA an
ANDA that would, if approved, allow Andrx to market a generic version of the <I>Cardizem LA </I>product.
Andrx&#146;s notice letter to Biovail alleged that its proposed product would not infringe United States
Patent Nos. 5,288,505 and 5,529,791, which are listed in the FDA Orange Book as covering <I>Cardizem
LA</I>, and that the &#145;505 patent was invalid. Under the terms of the Biovail Agreements, if a generic
drug company files an ANDA, Biovail has the first right to initiate a lawsuit, and Kos, in its
discretion, may initiate suit if Biovail elects not to file suit.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;On August&nbsp;10, 2005, a lawsuit against Andrx in Biovail&#146;s name was commenced in the United
States District Court for the District of Delaware (Civil Action No.&nbsp;05-586). The complaint
averred that Andrx&#146;s filing of its ANDA constituted infringement of the &#145;791 patent. Andrx&#146;s
Answer denied infringement of the &#145;791 patent and asserted affirmative defenses of invalidity. In
addition, Andrx counterclaimed for declaratory judgment of non-infringement and invalidity. Andrx
sought no monetary relief, other than recovery of attorney fees and costs.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Upon receiving a second Paragraph&nbsp;IV certification from Andrx directed to additional <I>Cardizem
LA </I>tablet strengths of 120, 180, 240, 300, and 360 mg added by an amendment to Andrx&#146;s ANDA, on
October&nbsp;14, 2005, a second complaint was filed in Biovail&#146;s name in the United States District
Court for the District of Delaware (Civil Action No.&nbsp;05-730). The complaint averred that Andrx&#146;s
Amended ANDA constituted infringement of the &#145;791 patent. Andrx&#146;s Answer denied infringement of the
&#145;791 patent and asserted affirmative defenses of invalidity. In addition, Andrx counterclaimed for
declaratory judgment of non-infringement and invalidity. Andrx sought no monetary relief, other
than recovery of attorney fees and costs.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Civil actions 05-586 and 05-730 have been consolidated by the Court for all purposes. Under
the current case schedule, fact and expert discovery is scheduled to close on September&nbsp;5, 2006,
and trial is scheduled for April&nbsp;9, 2007. These dates, however, may change.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;If the patents relating to <I>Cardizem LA </I>are invalid, unenforceable or not infringed, the
Company and Biovail would be unable to prevent Andrx from producing and marketing a generic version
of the <I>Cardizem LA </I>product, which could be sold at prices lower than those charged by the Company.
This would have a material adverse effect on the Company&#146;s business, operating results and
financial results. The Company and Biovail would also be unable to prevent other companies from
introducing similar generic products into the marketplace.
</DIV>

<DIV style="font-family: 'Times New Roman',Times,serif">

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B>Protection of Intellectual Property</B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Because the patent positions of pharmaceutical and biotechnology companies are highly
uncertain and involve complex legal and factual questions, the patents owned and licensed by the
Company, or any future patents, may not prevent other companies from developing similar or
therapeutically equivalent products or ensure that others will not be issued patents that may
prevent the sale of its products or require licensing and the payment of significant fees or
royalties. Furthermore, to the extent that any of Kos&#146; future products or methods are not
patentable, that such products or methods infringe upon the patents of third parties, or that the
Company&#146;s patents or future patents fail to give Kos an exclusive position in the subject matter
claimed by those patents, Kos will be adversely affected. Kos may be unable to avoid infringement
of third party patents and may have to obtain a license, defend an infringement action, or
challenge the validity of the patents in court. A license may be unavailable on terms and
conditions acceptable to Kos, if at all. Patent litigation is costly and time consuming, and Kos
may be unable to prevail in any such patent litigation or devote sufficient resources to even
pursue such litigation. If Kos does not obtain a license under such patents, is found liable for
infringement, or is not able to have such patents declared invalid, Kos may be liable for
significant money damages, may encounter significant delays in bringing products to market, or may
be precluded from participating in the manufacture, use, or sale of products or methods of
treatment requiring such licenses.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Kos also relies on trade secrets and other unpatented proprietary information in its product
development activities. To the extent that Kos maintains a competitive technological position, by
relying on trade secrets and unpatented know-how, such competitive technological position may be
compromised if others independently develop the same or similar technologies. Kos seeks to protect
trade secrets and proprietary knowledge in part through confidentiality agreements with its
employees, consultants, advisors, and collaborators. Nevertheless, these agreements may not
effectively prevent disclosure of Kos&#146; confidential information and may not provide the Company
with an adequate remedy in the event of unauthorized disclosure of such information. If Kos&#146;
employees, consultants, advisors, or collaborators develop inventions or processes independently
that may be applicable to Kos&#146; products under development, disputes may arise about ownership of
proprietary rights to those inventions and processes. Such inventions and processes will not
necessarily become the Company&#146;s property, but may remain the property of those persons or their
employers. Protracted and costly litigation could be necessary to enforce and determine the scope
of the Company&#146;s proprietary rights. Failure to obtain or maintain patent and trade secret
protection, for any reason, would have a material adverse effect on Kos.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Kos engages in collaborations, sponsored research agreements, and other arrangements with
academic researchers and institutions that have received and may receive funding from United States
government agencies. As a result of these arrangements, the United States government or certain
third parties may have rights in certain inventions developed during the course of the performance
of such collaborations and agreements as required by law or such agreements.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;<I>Niaspan</I>, <I>Advicor, Azmacort, Teveten </I>and <I>Kos </I>are the Company&#146;s principal registered trademarks,
although other applications for registration of trademarks and service marks are currently pending
in the Patent and Trademark Office and additional applications are in the process of being filed.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B>Manufacturing</B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Kos currently manufactures its <I>Niaspan </I>and <I>Advicor </I>products in a single manufacturing plant in
Edison, New Jersey that has been inspected and approved by the FDA for both products. In addition,
pursuant to the Barr License and Manufacturing Agreement, Barr stands ready to supply KLS
quantities of <I>Niaspan </I>500 mg, 750 mg and 1000 mg and <I>Advicor </I>500 mg/20 mg, 750 mg/20 mg and 1000
mg/20 mg, under or pursuant to the approval of Barr&#146;s ANDAs. On November&nbsp;11, 2005, Aeropharm
Technology, LLC (&#147;ATLLC&#148;), a wholly owned subsidiary of the Company, entered into an agreement with
a third party manufacturer (the &#147;Additional Supplier&#148;) for the manufacture and supply of <I>Niaspan</I>
500 mg. The agreement with the Additional Supplier also provides potential for the manufacture and
supply of <I>Niaspan </I>750 mg and 1000 mg upon agreement by the parties and conditioned upon validation
testing and approval satisfactory to ATLLC. The Additional Supplier&#146;s manufacturing facility has
been approved for <I>Niaspan </I>500 mg and the Additional Supplier began supply of <I>Niaspan </I>500 mg in
November&nbsp;2005.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Although both of the <I>Niaspan </I>and <I>Advicor </I>products have been approved for manufacture in the
Edison facility, Barr has a facility standing ready to manufacture <I>Niaspan</I>, and <I>Niaspan </I>500 mg has
been approved for manufacture in the Additional Supplier&#146;s facility, Kos or its third party
suppliers may identify inefficiencies or other problems that may exist in the manufacturing
process. Kos or its third party suppliers may need to further scale-up certain of their respective
current manufacturing processes to achieve production levels consistent with the commercial sale of
Kos&#146; products. Further, modifications to Kos&#146; or its third party suppliers facilities, systems and
procedures may be necessary to maintain capacity at a level sufficient to meet market demand or to
maintain compliance with cGMP regulations and other regulations prescribed by various regulatory
agencies, including the Occupational Safety and Health Administration and the Environmental
Protection Agency. The failure of the Company or its third party suppliers to successfully further
scale-up, expand in connection with manufacture for commercial sale, or modify its manufacturing
process, or to comply with cGMP regulations and other regulations could delay the approval of Kos&#146;
products under development or limit its ability to meet the demand for its products, any of which
would have a material adverse effect on the Company. Such occurrences may require Kos to acquire
alternative means of manufacturing its products, which may not be available to the Company on a
timely basis, on commercially practicable terms, or at all.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The Company obtains the <I>Azmacort </I>product pursuant to the <I>Azmacort </I>Supply Agreement, as amended
and assigned to Inyx, which is scheduled to remain in effect, subject to other terms, conditions
and provisions regarding early termination, until at least March&nbsp;31, 2015. As such, the Company
relies on Inyx to manufacture and supply adequate quantities of the finished <I>Azmacort </I>product.
Although there is one other company that is currently qualified to manufacture and supply the
<I>Azmacort </I>product, the Company does not have any contractual arrangement with such other entity. As
a result, the Company relies solely on Inyx to supply the Company with the <I>Azmacort </I>product, and
the Company cannot provide any assurances that Inyx will be able to produce sufficient quantities
of the <I>Azmacort </I>product to meet the Company&#146;s requirements.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The Company obtains the <I>Cardizem LA </I>product and the <I>Teveten </I>Products pursuant to a supply
agreement with Biovail and a supply agreement (the &#147;Solvay Supply Agreement&#148;) between Biovail and
Solvay Pharmaceuticals Marketing &#038; Licensing AG (&#147;Solvay&#148;), which was assigned to the Company
pursuant to the Biovail Agreements, respectively. The supply agreement for the <I>Cardizem LA </I>product
is scheduled to remain in effect, subject to other terms, conditions and provisions regarding early
termination, until at least the fourth anniversary of the generic entry of <I>Cardizem LA </I>into the
market and the Solvay Supply Agreement is scheduled to remain in effect, subject to other terms,
conditions and provisions of the Supply Agreement regarding early termination, until the patents of
the <I>Teveten </I>Products expire. As such, the Company relies on Biovail and Solvay to manufacture and
supply adequate quantities of the finished <I>Cardizem LA </I>product and <I>Teveten </I>Products. The Company
cannot provide any assurances that Biovail and Solvay will be able to produce sufficient quantities of the
<I>Cardizem LA </I>product and <I>Teveten </I>Products, respectively, to meet the Company&#146;s requirements.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B>Competition</B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The Company&#146;s products compete with currently existing or future prescription pharmaceuticals,
dietary supplements, vitamins and other over the counter therapies in the United States, Canada,
Europe, and elsewhere. Kos estimates that its existing <I>Niaspan </I>and <I>Advicor </I>prescriptions account
for approximately 3.4% of the total prescriptions currently being written in the United States for
cholesterol-altering pharmaceutical compounds and that its existing <I>Azmacort </I>prescriptions account
for approximately 8% of the total prescriptions currently being written in the United States for
inhaled corticosteroid products. Additionally, the Company&#146;s hypertension products <I>Cardizem LA,
Teveten </I>and <I>Teveten HCT </I>account for a prescription share of 1.9% of the calcium channel blocker and
0.7% of the angiotensin receptor blocker markets, respectively. Competition among
cholesterol-lowering pharmaceutical compounds, anti-hypertensive therapies and inhaled
corticosteroid therapies is intense. Competition among these products is based on, among other
things, efficacy, safety, reliability, availability, price, patent position, side effects,
promotion and advertising. In addition, academic institutions, government agencies, and other
public and private organizations conducting research may seek patent protection, discover new
drugs, or establish collaborative arrangements for drug research. Most of Kos&#146; competitors have
substantially greater financial, marketing, technical, and human resources than those available to
the Company, including combined field sales forces approximating 15,000 representatives currently
active within the dyslipidemia and respiratory markets, compared with the Company&#146;s approximately
750-person total field sales force and may be better equipped to develop, manufacture, advertise
and market products. Many of Kos&#146; competitors can afford print and TV direct-to-consumer
advertising (e.g., Crestor, Advair and Diovan), enjoy the advantage of greater patient awareness of
competing products, such as statins, and enjoy the cumulative benefits of prior marketing and
advertising efforts that pre-date Kos&#146; promotional efforts. The large size and rapid growth of the
cardiovascular and respiratory disease therapy markets continues to encourage new product entries.
Moreover, there are numerous manufacturers of niacin preparations indicated for use as vitamin or
dietary supplements or, in immediate-release form, for treatment of hyperlipidemia, as well as
numerous manufacturers of inhaled corticosteroids for the treatment of asthma, and numerous
manufacturers of anti-hypertensive treatments.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The Company&#146;s cardiovascular and respiratory products, when developed and marketed, will also
compete in most cases with well established products containing similar active ingredients already
being marketed by medium-sized and large pharmaceutical companies in the U.S.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B>Employees</B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;As of December&nbsp;31, 2005, Kos had 1,361 employees. No employee is represented by a union. Kos
believes its employee relations are good. Kos also regularly employs the services of outside
consultants with respect to regulatory, scientific, and certain administrative and commercial
matters. Kos expects to continue to require the services of such outside consultants.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B>ITEM 1A. RISK FACTORS.</B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt"><B>Market Acceptance and Sales Growth of </B><B><I>Niaspan </I></B><B>and </B><B><I>Advicor</I></B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The Company&#146;s success depends in large part upon its ability to successfully market and sell
increasing quantities of the <I>Niaspan </I>and <I>Advicor </I>products. The Company&#146;s ability to successfully
sell increasing quantities of the <I>Niaspan </I>product will depend significantly on the continued
acceptance of the <I>Niaspan </I>product by physicians and their patients despite the commercialization of
<I>Advicor</I>. As a consequence of the on-going commercialization of the <I>Advicor </I>product, which is a
combination product including <I>Niaspan </I>and lovastatin, a statin-class compound, prescription levels
for <I>Niaspan </I>may be adversely affected to the extent a significant number of physicians prescribe
<I>Advicor </I>as a substitute product for their patients who are currently taking <I>Niaspan</I>. Such
substitution could have an adverse effect on the growth of the combined revenue generated from the
sale of the Company&#146;s products. Further, there are numerous versions of niacin currently available
as a dietary supplement. The Company&#146;s ability to successfully sell increasing quantities of the
<I>Niaspan </I>and <I>Advicor </I>products may be affected by the substitution of niacin dietary supplements for
its products. There are also competing pharmaceutical companies, including Merck &#038; Co., Inc.,
Pfizer, Inc., and Roche that are currently conducting clinical trials on therapies to increase
levels of HDL cholesterol. Some of these companies are focusing on cholesterol-ester transfer
protein therapy as a mechanism to improve HDL, while others are focusing on extended release niacin
products and niacin-type mimetics. These new agents, if approved, would pose a significant
competitive threat to <I>Niaspan </I>and <I>Advicor</I>, as they target similar mechanisms of action and/or
similar cardiovascular treatment objectives. Sales could also be affected by competition from the
sale of other statin products and other cholesterol therapies. The Company believes that
intolerable flushing and potential liver toxicity associated with the currently available dietary
supplement formulations of niacin are the principal reasons why physicians generally have been
reluctant to prescribe or recommend such formulations. Flushing episodes are often characterized
by facial redness, tingling or rash, and are a side effect that often occurs when humans ingest
niacin. Currently available formulations of niacin generally result either, in the case of
immediate-release niacin, in multiple flushing episodes, or, in the case of sustained-release
niacin, in potential liver toxicity. Although most patients taking either the <I>Niaspan </I>or <I>Advicor</I>
product will sometimes flush, the formulation and dosing regimen for <I>Niaspan </I>and <I>Advicor </I>have been
designed to maximize patient acceptance and minimize the occurrence of flushing and liver toxicity.
If, however, a significant number of patients using the <I>Niaspan </I>or <I>Advicor </I>products were to suffer
episodes of flushing that they consider intolerable or to suffer other side effects, physicians may
discontinue prescribing the <I>Niaspan </I>or <I>Advicor </I>products or patients may stop taking <I>Niaspan </I>or
<I>Advicor</I>, which would have a material adverse effect on the Company. Unanticipated side effects or
unfavorable publicity concerning the <I>Niaspan </I>or <I>Advicor </I>products, or any other product
incorporating technology similar to that used in the <I>Niaspan </I>or <I>Advicor </I>products, also could have
an adverse effect on the Company&#146;s ability to (1)&nbsp;maintain and/or obtain regulatory approvals or
(2)&nbsp;to achieve acceptance by prescribing physicians, managed care providers, or patients, any of
which would have a material adverse effect on the Company.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The Company&#146;s ability to successfully sell increasing quantities of the <I>Advicor </I>product will
depend significantly on the continued and increasing acceptance of the <I>Advicor </I>product by
physicians and their patients. If a significant number of patients using the <I>Advicor </I>product were
to suffer episodes of flushing that they consider intolerable or to suffer more serious side
effects, such as rhabdomyolysis or myopathy, physicians may discontinue prescribing the <I>Advicor</I>
product or patients may stop taking <I>Advicor</I>, which would have a material adverse effect on the
Company. Rhabdomyolysis is a rare disease in which serious muscle damage results in release of
muscle cell contents into the bloodstream, which may be fatal.
</DIV>

<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt">22
</DIV>

<!-- PAGEBREAK -->
<P><HR noshade><P>
<H5 align="left" style="page-break-before:always"><A HREF="#tocpage">Table of Contents</A></H5><P>

<DIV style="font-family: 'Times New Roman',Times,serif">

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Myopathy is a disorder of muscle tissue or muscles that can result from endocrine disorders,
metabolic disorders, infection or inflammation of the muscles, and from certain drugs. There have
been several cases where <I>Niaspan </I>(one of the principal ingredients in <I>Advicor</I>) or <I>Advicor </I>have been
identified as possible causes of myopathy.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;In addition, <I>Advicor </I>may prove to be difficult to successfully sell beyond current levels
because the market for cholesterol therapies is dominated by competitors with significantly larger
sales forces and with significantly greater marketing resources than those available to the
Company. Companies competing for the sales of the Company&#146;s products boast well-organized
direct-to-consumer advertising and strong physician and patient promotional efforts. Further,
<I>Advicor </I>is a combination of two well-known cholesterol drugs, <I>Niaspan </I>and lovastatin, that have
been available for a significant period of time. Although the combination of these drugs is highly
effective in improving the major components of cholesterol, it is possible that physicians may not
prescribe <I>Advicor </I>because its components are not as new as more recently introduced compounds, such
as the potent statin products marketed by the Company&#146;s competitors. Also, because <I>Advicor </I>is a
combination of two currently available drugs, <I>Advicor </I>has been approved by the FDA for the
improvement of cholesterol disorders in patients who were not able to achieve desired cholesterol
improvements by taking either <I>Niaspan </I>or lovastatin alone. Consequently, although such an approved
treatment indication is standard for combination drugs such as <I>Advicor</I>, it is possible that
physicians will not prescribe <I>Advicor </I>until they have first prescribed either <I>Niaspan</I>, lovastatin,
or another statin alone and subsequently determined that their patients need <I>Advicor </I>to achieve
desired cholesterol improvements.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Similarly, the Company&#146;s ability to successfully sell increasing quantities of the <I>Advicor</I>
product may be adversely affected by, among other things, (1)&nbsp;the November&nbsp;2002 launch of Zetia,
part of a new class of cholesterol-lowering agents, (2)&nbsp;the September&nbsp;2003 launch of Crestor, a
highly powerful statin product, or (3)&nbsp;the August&nbsp;2004 introduction of Vytorin, a new, dual
component therapy possessing significant potency with respect to reducing LDL cholesterol.
Although Zetia, Crestor and Vytorin possess limited potency with respect to increasing HDL
cholesterol, an increased focus of the medical community in treating LDL cholesterol could
adversely affect the utilization of the Company&#146;s products, in which the differentiated feature is
modulating other lipid risk factors that have been implicated in heart disease, but are not as well
established as LDL cholesterol in contributing to coronary heart disease. Zetia and Vytorin are
being marketed by Merck &#038; Co., Inc./Schering-Plough Pharmaceuticals, which is a joint venture
between Merck &#038; Co., Inc. and Schering-Plough Corporation, and Crestor is marketed by AstraZeneca
Pharmaceuticals LP. Merck &#038; Co., Inc./Schering-Plough Pharmaceuticals and AstraZeneca
Pharmaceuticals LP are competitors with substantially greater resources than Kos. Zetia is part of
a new class of cholesterol-lowering agents that inhibit the intestinal absorption of cholesterol.
Zetia is highly effective in reducing LDL cholesterol, but it has minimal effect in reducing HDL
cholesterol. Crestor is a type of statin drug that is highly effective in reducing LDL
cholesterol, but is less effective in modifying HDL cholesterol, triglycerides and other forms of
cholesterol. Vytorin is a combination product of the statin Zocor and Zetia that possesses
significant potency with respect to reducing LDL cholesterol, but is less effective in increasing
HDL cholesterol. In late 2004, a new formulation of the fibrate agent Tricor (fenofibrate) was
launched by Abbott Laboratories, an agent that primarily affects triglyceride levels, but also
increases HDL cholesterol. A new fenofibrate product, Antara, was also launched by Reliant
Pharmaceuticals, LLC in February&nbsp;2005. These new agents also pose a competitive threat to <I>Niaspan</I>
as they affect the two lipoproteins also impacted by <I>Niaspan</I>. Additionally, in November&nbsp;2004, the
FDA approved Omacor, an omega 3 fatty acid product introduced by Reliant Pharmaceuticals, LLC for
the treatment of patients with very high levels of triglycerides. Omacor competes with <I>Niaspan </I>in
its ability to lower triglyceride levels. Further, there are at least ten versions of generic
lovastatin, one of the components of <I>Advicor</I>, that have been launched into the large and growing
market for cholesterol therapies, which could adversely affect demand for <I>Advicor</I>. The sale of
other well-known statin therapies and other cholesterol drugs is often driven by competitors with
stronger promotion and name recognition for their products. Consequently, the Company&#146;s effort to
sell increasing quantities of the <I>Advicor </I>product may be unsuccessful.
</DIV>

<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt">23
</DIV>

<DIV style="font-family: 'Times New Roman',Times,serif">

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B>Sales Growth of </B><B><I>Azmacort; </I></B><B>Technological Feasibility of </B><B><I>Azmacort </I></B><B>HFA formulation</B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;There can be no assurance that the Company will be able to continue to profitably manage the
<I>Azmacort </I>product utilizing the Company&#146;s sales and marketing campaigns without substantial
expenses, delays or other operational or financial problems. The Company&#146;s effort to commercialize
the <I>Azmacort </I>product may require management to devote a disproportionate amount of its resources
toward the commercialization and growth of <I>Azmacort</I>, which may divert management, sales personnel
and financial resources from the Company&#146;s other products. This could have a material adverse
effect on the Company&#146;s business, operating results and financial condition. There can be no
assurance that <I>Azmacort </I>will continue to achieve the level of revenues that were derived from its
sale prior to Kos&#146; acquisition of <I>Azmacort</I>, or that the Company will continue to achieve its
anticipated revenues and earnings. Prior to the Company&#146;s acquisition of <I>Azmacort</I>, the product had
experienced declining sales levels and increased competition from other products. The Company&#146;s
failure to manage the commercialization of the <I>Azmacort </I>product in a way that continues to reverse
the declining trend in prescriptions of such product, the Company&#146;s failure to achieve substantial
growth in the sales of the <I>Azmacort </I>product, or the Company&#146;s failure to source sufficient
quantities of <I>Azmacort </I>from Inyx, could have a material adverse effect on the Company&#146;s business
and financial condition.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;In addition, <I>Azmacort </I>may prove difficult to successfully sell beyond current levels due to
the competitive nature of the marketplace in which the product competes. The competitive arena is
dominated by Glaxo SmithKline&#146;s Advair, supported by a significantly larger sales force than that
which supports <I>Azmacort</I>. In addition, in 2005 the FDA approved Schering-Plough&#146;s new inhaled
cortico-steroid agent Asmanex, which is now being marketed in the United States.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The Montreal Protocol on Substances that Deplete the Ozone Layer is an international treaty
under which the production and consumption of ozone-depleting substances is being phased out
worldwide. Under this Protocol, codified by the United States Congress into law in Title VI of the
Clean Air Act, the production of CFCs in the United States was banned as of January&nbsp;1, 1996, unless
a specific exemption is approved annually by the international parties to the Protocol. To comply
with the Clean Air Act and the Protocol, the United States will eventually need to phase out all
CFC-propelled Metered Dose Inhalers. The HFA formulation of <I>Azmacort </I>does not use a CFC-based
propellant. Thus, to the Company&#146;s knowledge, <I>Azamcort </I>HFA should not contribute to the depletion
of the Earth&#146;s ozone layer. Although <I>Azmacort </I>HFA has received an &#147;approvable&#148; letter from the FDA,
the <I>Azmacort </I>HFA formulation was not technological feasible as of the date of the Aventis
Agreements. Among the technological matters to be resolved are manufacturing controls and evidence
of dose proportionality between the 75 &#181;g and 225 &#181;g dosage formulations. There can be no
assurance that the Company will be able to make the <I>Azmacort </I>HFA formulation technologically
feasible prior to an FDA decision to phase out <I>Azmacort </I>CFC as of a particular date in the future.
Failure to obtain FDA approval and bring <I>Azmacort </I>HFA to market prior to, or contemporaneously
with, the FDA&#146;s phase out of <I>Azmacort </I>CFC in the United States could have a material adverse effect
on the Company&#146;s business, operating results and financial condition.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B>Integration and Sales Growth of </B><B><I>Cardizem LA, Teveten, </I></B><B>and </B><B><I>Teveten HCT </I></B><B>Products</B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;There can be no assurance that the Company will be able to sustainably manage and integrate
the <I>Cardizem LA</I>, <I>Teveten, </I>and <I>Teveten HCT </I>products into the Company&#146;s sales and marketing campaigns
without substantial expenses, delays or other operational or financial problems. The integration
of these products into the Company&#146;s business may prove to be expensive and time consuming and
require significant management attention, which may divert management, sales personnel and
financial resources from the Company&#146;s other products. This could have a material adverse effect
on the Company&#146;s business, operating results and financial condition. The Company&#146;s failure to
integrate the products or sales personnel and realize the expected benefits from its acquisitions
could have a material adverse effect on the Company&#146;s business, operating results and financial
condition.
</DIV>

<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt">24
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;In addition, <I>Teveten </I>and <I>Teveten HCT </I>may prove difficult to successfully sell beyond current
levels due to the competitive nature of the marketplace in which the products compete and to
limited managed care coverage. The competitive arena is dominated by large players such as

(Atacand) and Bristol Myers Squibb Company (Avapro), all of whom have large sales organizations to

efforts of Sankyo Co., Ltd and Forest Pharmaceuticals, Inc. have also recently entered the market
utilizing significantly sized sales forces and strong marketing messages.

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B>Patents and Trademarks</B>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The Company&#146;s ability to commercialize any of its products under development will depend, in

preserve trade secrets, and operate without infringing on the proprietary rights of third parties.
In addition, the patents that the Company has had issued or for which Kos has applied relating to
<I>Niaspan</I>, <I>Advicor </I>and certain of the Company&#146;s products under development are based on, among other
things, the extended-release nature of the niacin active ingredient. If the indications treated by
<I>Niaspan</I>, <I>Advicor </I>and such other products under development could be treated using drugs without
such extended-release properties, such patents and patent applications may not prevent the use of
other niacin-based drugs for the treatment of such indications, which would have a material adverse
effect on the Company. Further, the Company would be adversely affected if:

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;In general, the United States Patent and Trademark Office, referred to as PTO, patents and
patent applications that Kos owns or licenses are directed to formulations and their methods of
use. Protection of the chemical entity is not available for the active ingredient in <I>Niaspan</I>, or
either of the active ingredients in <I>Advicor</I>. Moreover, the active ingredient in <I>Niaspan</I>, niacin,
is currently sold in the United States and other markets for lipid altering indications and for
other uses. The additional active ingredient in <I>Advicor</I>, lovastatin, is also currently sold in the
United States and other markets for lipid altering indications. Even in jurisdictions where the
use of the active ingredients in <I>Niaspan </I>and <I>Advicor </I>for lipid altering and other indications may
be covered by the claims of method-of-use or composition patents that Kos owns or licenses,
off-label or dietary supplement sales might occur, especially if another company markets the active
ingredient at a price that is less than the price of <I>Niaspan </I>or <I>Advicor</I>, thereby potentially
reducing the sales of such products.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The PTO has issued United States Patent numbers 6,129,930, 6,080,428, 6,406,715, 6,676,967,
6,746,691 and 6,818,229 to the Company with claims related to <I>Niaspan&#146;s </I>and <I>Advicor&#146;s </I>composition
and method-of-use consistent with the recommended once-a-day regime. On February&nbsp;7, 1997, the
Company entered into an agreement with a generic manufacturer pursuant to which the Company and the
manufacturer granted cross-licenses to each other under their respective patents. The Company has
subsequently purchased the two patents that were the subject of the cross-license agreement and
agreed to continue paying a royalty to the manufacturer on terms similar to those contained in the
cross-license agreement.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Certain of the Company&#146;s marketed products are covered by one or more patents that are
identified and listed with the FDA. Under provisions of the Hatch Waxman Act, 21 USC 1 et seq., a
company that
</DIV>

<DIV style="font-family: 'Times New Roman',Times,serif">

generic company may indicate that it will not sell a generic version of the Company&#146;s marketed
product until the patent(s) covering the marketed products expire, or that the generic manufacturer
believes that the patent(s) listed by the Company are invalid, unenforceable or would not be

infringement litigation to defend the patent(s) covering the Company&#146;s marketed product or, if no
such patent litigation is brought or if the patent(s) are deemed invalid, unenforceable or not
infringed, the introduction of a generic version of the Company&#146;s marketed products prior to the
expiration of the patent(s) covering the Company&#146;s marketed products, which could have a material

costly and time consuming administrative review by antitrust enforcement agencies, such as the
Federal Trade Commission and Department of Justice Antitrust Division, and may include agency
investigation and potential civil or administrative litigation.

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;On April&nbsp;12, 2005, the Company entered into the Duramed Co-promotion Agreement, the Barr
License and Manufacturing Agreement, and the Barr Settlement and License Agreement, referred to as
the Barr Agreements, with Barr. The Barr Agreements were entered into at about the same time as the
resolution of the patent litigation involving the Company&#146;s <I>Niaspan </I>products. Under the Barr
License and Manufacturing Agreement, Barr agreed to stand ready to supply KLS, a wholly-owned
subsidiary of Kos, certain quantities of <I>Niaspan </I>and <I>Advicor </I>tablets. Barr approved, and granted
KLS a license to use, market and sell, at a future date, Barr&#146;s versions of the Company&#146;s current
<I>Niaspan </I>and <I>Advicor </I>products in the United States. Barr received an initial license fee and
receives quarterly payments to remain compliant with FDA current good manufacturing practices and
to stand ready to meet the Company&#146;s manufacturing requirements on short notice.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The Barr Settlement and License Agreement sets forth the terms of the settlement of the
litigation between Kos and Barr. It permits Barr to launch a generic version of <I>Niaspan </I>and
<I>Advicor</I>, as well as future dosage formulations, strengths or modified versions of the products,
under terms of an exclusive license commencing on September&nbsp;20, 2013, approximately four years
earlier than the last-to-expire KLS patent. Pursuant to the Duramed Co-promotion Agreement,
Duramed Pharmaceuticals, Inc., referred to as Duramed, an affiliate of Barr, promotes the Company&#146;s
<I>Niaspan </I>and <I>Advicor </I>products to physicians who mostly specialize in women&#146;s healthcare. In
consideration of Duramed&#146;s performance of its co-promotion obligations, the Company pays Duramed
royalties subject to certain sales caps.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;On April&nbsp;22, 2005, the Company provided to the Federal Trade Commission and Department of
Justice Anti-trust Division the notifications of the settlement with Barr as required under Section
1112(a) of the Medicare Prescription Drug, Improvement and Modernization Act of 2003. The
settlement with Barr and the other arrangements with Barr and its affiliates may be reviewed by
anti-trust enforcement agencies, such as the Federal Trade Commission and Department of Justice
Anti-trust Division. There can be no assurances that governmental authorities will not seek to
challenge the settlement with Barr or that a competitor, customer or other third party will not
initiate a private action under antitrust or other laws challenging the settlement with Barr. The
Company may not prevail in any such challenges or litigation and, in any event, may incur
significant costs in the event of an investigation or in defending any action under antitrust laws.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Because the patent positions of pharmaceutical and biotechnology companies are highly
uncertain and involve complex legal and factual questions, the patents that Kos owns and licenses,
or any future patents, may not prevent other companies from developing competing products or ensure
that others will not be issued patents that may prevent the sale of the Company&#146;s products or
require licensing and the payment of significant fees or royalties. Furthermore, to the extent
that (1)&nbsp;any of the Company&#146;s future products or methods are not patentable, (2)&nbsp;such products or
methods infringe upon the patents of third parties, or (3)&nbsp;the Company&#146;s patents or future patents
fail to give Kos an exclusive position in the subject matter to which those patents relate, the
Company will be adversely affected. The Company may be
</DIV>

defend an infringement action and challenge the validity of the patents in court. A license may be

or devote sufficient resources to even pursue such litigation. If the Company does not obtain a

such patents declared invalid, the Company may be liable for significant money damages, may
encounter significant delays in bringing products to market, or may be precluded from participating
in the manufacture, use, or sale of products or methods of treatment requiring such licenses.

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Third parties own patents relating to some of the Company&#146;s marketed products. The Company
relies on such third parties to enforce the validity of the patents and defend any infringements on
the patents. If the third parties do not enforce the patents, the Company would be unable to

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;On July&nbsp;7, 2005, the Company received notice from Biovail that Andrx had filed with the FDA an
ANDA that would, if approved, allow Andrx to market a generic version of the <I>Cardizem LA </I>product.
Andrx&#146;s notice letter to Biovail alleged that its proposed product would not infringe United States
Patent Nos. 5,288,505 and 5,529,791, which are listed in the FDA Orange Book as covering <I>Cardizem
LA</I>, or alternatively that those patents were invalid. Under the terms of the Biovail Agreements, if
a generic drug company files an ANDA, Biovail has the first right to initiate a lawsuit, and Kos,
in its discretion, may initiate suit if Biovail elects not to file suit.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;On August&nbsp;10, 2005, a lawsuit against Andrx in Biovail&#146;s name was commenced in the United
States District Court for the District of Delaware. The complaint stated that Andrx&#146;s filing of
its ANDA infringed the &#145;791 patent. Andrx&#146;s Answer denied infringement of the &#145;791 patent and
asserted affirmative defenses of invalidity, meaning even if Andrx did infringe the &#145;791 patent,
Andrx asserted the patent is invalid. In addition, Andrx counterclaimed for declaratory judgment
of non-infringement and invalidity. Andrx sought no monetary relief, other than recovery of
attorneys&#146; fees and costs.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Upon receiving a second Paragraph&nbsp;IV certification from Andrx directed to additional <I>Cardizem
LA </I>tablet strengths of 120, 180, 240, 300, and 360 mg added by amendment to Andrx&#146;s ANDA (&#147;Amended
Andrx ANDA&#148;), on October&nbsp;14, 2005, a second complaint was filed in Biovail&#146;s name (Civil Action No.
05-730) in the United States District Court for the District of Delaware. The complaint asserted
that Andrx&#146;s Amended ANDA infringed the &#145;791 patent. Andrx&#146;s Answer denied infringement of the
&#145;791 patent and asserted affirmative defenses of invalidity, meaning even if Andrx did infringe the
&#145;791 patent, Andrx asserted the patent is invalid. In addition, Andrx counterclaimed for
declaratory judgment of non-infringement and invalidity. Andrx sought no monetary relief, other
than recovery of attorneys&#146; fees and costs.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Civil actions 05-586 and 05-730 have been consolidated by the Court for all purposes. Under
the current case schedule, fact and expert discovery is scheduled to close on September&nbsp;5, 2006,
and trial is scheduled for April&nbsp;9, 2007. These dates, however, may change.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;If the patents relating to <I>Cardizem LA </I>are invalid, unenforceable or not infringed, the
Company and Biovail would be unable to prevent Andrx from producing and marketing a generic version
of the <I>Cardizem LA </I>product, which could be sold at prices lower than those the Company charges.
This would have a material adverse effect on the Company&#146;s business, operating results and
financial condition. The Company and Biovail would also be unable to prevent other companies from
introducing similar generic products into the marketplace.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B>Future Capital Needs; Uncertainty of Additional Funding</B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The Company could borrow from Michael Jaharis, the Company&#146;s principal shareholder, Director
and Chairman Emeritus and/or his transferees up to $30&nbsp;million under an agreement currently in
place through June&nbsp;30, 2008. However, before the Company can borrow from Mr.&nbsp;Jaharis, the
conditions of borrowings must be satisfied including, without limitation, that the death of lender
shall not have occurred, the lender, his spouse, children and entities they control continue to own
at least 40% of Common Stock of the Company, and no material adverse change shall have occurred to
the Company or its financial condition. The repayment of funds borrowed from Mr.&nbsp;Jaharis is secured
by the pledge of all of the Company&#146;s assets to him. As a result, if the Company is unable to repay
the loans as they become due, the Company could be forced to liquidate the Company&#146;s assets or
transfer all of such assets to Mr.&nbsp;Jaharis and his transferees. On January&nbsp;10, 2005, Mr.&nbsp;Jaharis
gifted his rights and obligations under the facility to his wife.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The Company has spent, and will continue to be required to spend, substantial funds to
continue research and development activities, including clinical trials of the Company&#146;s products
under development, and to commercialize the <I>Niaspan, Advicor, Azmacort, Teveten, Teveten HCT </I>and
<I>Cardizem LA </I>products, referred to as the Product Portfolio, products and the Company&#146;s other
products under development, if regulatory approvals are obtained for such products under
development.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The Company also anticipates that it will have to incur significant capital expenditures
related to the expansion of its production and administrative facilities. The Company believes
that it has sufficient resources, including funds available to it under a line of credit from Mrs.
Jaharis, to fund the Company&#146;s operations through at least the next twelve months. Even with the
proceeds from these sources, however, the Company may need or elect to raise additional capital
prior to such date. The Company&#146;s ability to fund its operating requirements and maintain an
adequate level of working capital for the next twelve months will depend primarily on: (1)&nbsp;the
near-term commercial success of its Product Portfolio; (2)&nbsp;its ability to meet the conditions
necessary to obtain funding under its line of credit, including, without limitation, that the death
of Mr.&nbsp;Jaharis shall not have occurred, the lender, his spouse, children and entities they control
continue to own at least 40% of Common Stock of the Company, and no material adverse change shall
have occurred to the Company or its financial condition; (3)&nbsp;the problems, delays, expenses and
complications frequently encountered by companies at this stage of development; (4)&nbsp;the progress of
the Company&#146;s research, development, and clinical trial programs; (5)&nbsp;the costs and timing of
seeking regulatory approvals of the Company&#146;s products under development; (6)&nbsp;the Company&#146;s ability
to obtain such regulatory approvals in the United States and abroad; (7)&nbsp;costs associated with
filing, prosecuting, defending, litigating and enforcing patent claims and other intellectual
property rights; (8)&nbsp;the Company&#146;s ability to manufacture and supply (or have manufactured and
supplied) sufficient quantities of the products comprising its Product Portfolio; (9)&nbsp;the extent
and terms of any collaborative research, manufacturing, marketing, joint venture or other
arrangements; (10)&nbsp;the Company&#146;s ability to maintain coverage and reimbursement of its products by
government agencies and the effects of the loss of such coverage and reimbursement with such
agencies, such as the Centers for Medicare and Medicaid Services; (11)&nbsp;reduction of inventory
levels in the Company&#146;s distribution channels as a result of inventory management agreements; (12)
the Company&#146;s ability to ensure compliance with prescription drug sales and marketing laws and
regulations; and (13)&nbsp;changes in economic, regulatory, or competitive conditions or the Company&#146;s
planned business. Estimates about the adequacy of funding for the Company&#146;s activities are based on
certain assumptions, including assumptions regarding the marketing and sales success of the
Company&#146;s Product Portfolio the outcome of litigation related to the Company&#146;s intellectual
property rights, including the litigation between Andrx and Biovail relating to the <I>Cardizem</I>
product, and that testing and regulatory procedures relating to the Company&#146;s other products under
development can be conducted at projected costs and within projected time frames. To the extent
these assumptions prove to be inaccurate, the Company may have insufficient resources to fund its
operations as currently planned.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;To satisfy the Company&#146;s capital requirements, it may seek to raise funds in the public or
private capital markets. The Company&#146;s ability to raise additional funds in the public markets may
be adversely affected (1)&nbsp;if sales of the products comprising its Product Portfolio do not increase
rapidly; (2)&nbsp;if the results of the clinical trials for the Company&#146;s products under development are
not favorable; or (3)&nbsp;if regulatory approval for any of the Company&#146;s products under development is
not obtained. The Company may seek additional funding through corporate collaborations and other
financing vehicles. If adequate funds are not available to the Company, or if available, their
terms are unacceptable, the Company may be required to significantly reduce its marketing
activities for one or more of the products comprising its Product Portfolio or curtail
significantly one or more of the Company&#146;s research or development programs. In addition, the
Company may be required to obtain funds through arrangements with future collaborative partners or
others that may require the Company to relinquish rights to one or more of the products comprising
its Product Portfolio or to some or all of the Company&#146;s technologies or products under development
or take significant cost-reducing actions or both. If the Company is successful in obtaining
additional financing, the terms of the financing may have the effect of diluting or adversely
affecting the holdings or the rights of the holders of the Company&#146;s securities.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B>Registration Rights</B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The Company has granted certain registration rights to its controlling shareholder, Mr.
Jaharis, his spouse, and their transferees, and to Kos Investments, Inc. and Kos Holdings, Inc.,
which entitle such persons and entities to cause the Company to effect an unlimited number of
registrations under the Securities Act of 1933 (the &#147;Securities Act&#148;) of sales of up to an
aggregate of 23,503,367 shares of the Company&#146;s Common Stock owned or controlled by such persons or
entities or that could be acquired by such person or entities upon exercise or conversion of
securities they currently hold. These registration rights generally would also permit the holders
of such rights to include shares in any registration statement that the Company otherwise filed. By
exercising these registration rights, these persons and entities could cause a large number of
shares to be registered and become freely tradeable without restrictions under the Securities Act
(except for those purchased in the offering by the Company&#146;s affiliates) immediately upon the
effectiveness of such registration. Such sales may have an adverse effect on the market price of
the Company&#146;s Common Stock and could impair the Company&#146;s ability to raise additional capital.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B>Limited Sales and Marketing Resources</B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Although the products comprising the Product Portfolio are marketed by the Company&#146;s own sales
forces or through co-promotion or commercialization partners, substantial resources will continue
to be required for Kos to promote the sale of its products. Because its current marketing resources
are limited, the Company may be unable to devote sufficient resources to any one or more of its
products, or to the Company&#146;s products under development or to be acquired or licensed, or to
achieve increasing market acceptance of such products in the highly competitive marketplace in
which the Company&#146;s products compete. The Company&#146;s failure to expend the resources to adequately
promote the existing promoted products or its other products under development would have a
material adverse effect on the Company&#146;s business and results of operations.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Moreover, because the Company has fewer sales persons than its competitors, the Company&#146;s
sales force or its co-promotion and commercialization partners&#146; sales forces may be unable to
detail successfully physicians who prescribe competitor therapies or other medications. The Company
may not be able to retain its current sales representatives. The Company&#146;s co-promotion and
commercialization partners&#146; may not be able to retain their current sales representatives. Even if
the Company hires additional representatives, they may not be immediately effective in promoting
the sale of the Company&#146;s existing products. The Company&#146;s competitors may effectively counter-act
the marketing efforts of the Company and its co-promotion and commercialization partners. As a
result, Kos may be unable to generate significantly increased sales of the existing promoted
products. The failure of the Company to generate increased sales of the existing products would have a material adverse effect on
operating results.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B>Control by Existing Shareholder</B>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Michael Jaharis, the Company&#146;s principal shareholder and a director, beneficially owns,

Stock as of December&nbsp;31, 2005, or approximately 52% of the Common Stock outstanding. Accordingly,
Mr.&nbsp;Jaharis can control the outcome of certain shareholder votes, including votes concerning the
election of directors, the adoption or amendment of provisions in the Company&#146;s Articles of
Incorporation, and the approval of mergers and other significant corporate transactions. This level
of concentrated ownership by one person may have the effect of delaying or preventing a change in
the management or voting control of Kos.

cholesterol-altering pharmaceutical compounds. In addition, Zetia, part of a new class of
cholesterol-lowering agents that inhibit the intestinal absorption of cholesterol, was launched in
November&nbsp;2002 by Merck &#38; Co.,
Inc./Schering-Plough Pharmaceuticals. Crestor, a highly powerful statin product,
was launched during September&nbsp;2003 by AstraZeneca Pharmaceuticals LP. Vytorin, a combination
product of the statin Zocor and Zetia, was launched in July&nbsp;2004 by
Merck &#38; Co., Inc./Schering-Plough

Inc. and Schering-Plough Corporation. Both
Merck &#38; Co.,
Inc./Schering-Plough Pharmaceuticals and AstraZeneca Pharmaceuticals LP are competitors who have
substantially greater resources than those available to Kos. Finally, there are at least nine
versions of generic lovastatin, one of the components of <I>Advicor</I>, currently being marketed in the
cholesterol market. The existence of distributors of generic lovastatin and the competitive
environment they create may inhibit the <I>Niaspan </I>and <I>Advicor </I>products from competing successfully or
from achieving increased sales. Further, there is ongoing discussion at the FDA concerning the
potential approval of lovastatin as an over-the-counter drug. The sheer size and rapid growth of
the patient population for which cholesterol therapies are indicated, continue to induce new entry
of competing cardiovascular and cholesterol products.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Moreover, the active ingredient in <I>Niaspan</I>, niacin, is available in several other
formulations, most of which do not require a prescription. Although the Company believes that there
are no other currently available niacin formulations that the FDA has approved specifically for
once-a-day dosing, physicians may decide to prescribe or recommend some of these unapproved niacin
formulations, using the <I>Niaspan</I>

<DIV style="font-family: 'Times New Roman',Times,serif">

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B>International Commercialization Agreement with Merck KGaA</B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;On October&nbsp;23, 2002, the Company signed an exclusive international commercialization agreement
with Merck KGaA to market the <I>Niaspan </I>and <I>Advicor </I>products outside the United States, Canada and
Japan. Under the terms of the Merck Agreement, Merck KGaA will provide Kos up to $61&nbsp;million in
licensing, milestone and reimbursement payments. Through December&nbsp;31, 2005, Kos has received $20.0
million in upfront, reimbursement, and milestone payments from Merck KGaA, including $15.0&nbsp;million
of upfront and reimbursement payments. Kos is responsible for obtaining initial marketing
authorization in all European countries and the supply and manufacturing of the products.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Pursuant to certain provisions of the Merck Agreement, if marketing authorization is not
obtained for <I>Niaspan </I>and <I>Advicor </I>in certain countries and within certain timeframes, Kos will be
required to refund to Merck KGaA up to $3.8&nbsp;million of the upfront payments. In addition, other
milestone payments payable to Kos under the Merck Agreement are dependent on the achievement of
marketing authorization of <I>Niaspan </I>in certain countries, the achievement of marketing authorization
of <I>Advicor </I>in certain countries, and achieving certain net sales thresholds of <I>Niaspan </I>and <I>Advicor</I>.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;In 1999, Kos received marketing approval for <I>Niaspan </I>in the United Kingdom, and in 2002,
submitted <I>Advicor </I>for marketing approval in the United Kingdom. In November&nbsp;2003, Merck KGaA
launched the sale of <I>Niaspan </I>in the United Kingdom. With this launch and the December&nbsp;2003
completion of the then European Union&#146;s Mutual Recognition Procedure, referred to as MRP, <I>Niaspan</I>
has been registered in a total of 14 European markets, including such other markets as France,
Germany, Austria, Ireland, Belgium and the Netherlands. The product has now been launched in 11 of
these countries. Merck KGaA and Kos also plan to initiate a Repeat Use MRP for the additional
European countries that were added to the European Union in 2004. In addition, <I>Niaspan </I>has been
approved in other countries outside the European Union, including Lebanon and certain Latin
American countries. There is no certainty that further marketing authorization for the <I>Niaspan</I>
product and final authorization for the <I>Advicor </I>product will be obtained under the MRP, or, with
respect to <I>Advicor</I>, at all, and there could be delays in the granting of such authorizations.
Consequently, Kos may be required to reimburse Merck KGaA for up to $3.8&nbsp;million of the upfront
payments and may not receive any further milestone payments under the Merck Agreement.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B>No Assurance of Adequate Third-Party Reimbursement</B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The Company&#146;s ability to commercialize successfully its products under development is
dependent in part on the extent to which appropriate levels of reimbursement for the <I>Niaspan</I>,
<I>Advicor, Azmacort</I>, <I>Cardizem LA, Teveten </I>and <I>Teveten HCT </I>products are obtained from government
authorities, private health insurers, and managed care organizations such as health maintenance
organizations or HMOs. The Company estimates that, through December&nbsp;31, 2005, approximately $1.4
billion, or 74%, of the Company&#146;s cumulative <I>Niaspan</I>, <I>Advicor, Azmacort</I>, <I>Cardizem LA</I>, <I>Teveten </I>and
<I>Teveten HCT </I>revenue has been subjected to the reimbursement rules of such organizations. Managed
care organizations and other third-party payors are increasingly challenging the pricing of
pharmaceutical products. The trend toward managed healthcare in the United States, the growth of
organizations such as HMOs, and legislative and regulatory proposals to reform healthcare and
government programs providing pharmaceutical coverage could significantly influence the purchase of
pharmaceutical products, resulting in lower prices and reduced demand for <I>Niaspan</I>, <I>Advicor,
Azmacort</I>, <I>Cardizem LA, Teveten </I>and <I>Teveten HCT </I>products, or the Company&#146;s products under
development. Such cost containment measures and potential legislative and regulatory reform could
affect the Company&#146;s ability to sell <I>Niaspan</I>, <I>Advicor, Azmacort</I>, <I>Cardizem LA</I>, <I>Teveten, </I>and <I>Teveten
HCT </I>or its products under development and may have a material adverse effect on the Company.
Significant uncertainty exists about the reimbursement and coverage status of newly approved
pharmaceutical products. Although the Company has obtained approvals for reimbursement and coverage
for the cost of <I>Niaspan</I>, <I>Advicor, Azmacort</I>, <I>Cardizem LA, Teveten, </I>and <I>Teveten HCT </I>products from
many third-party payors, such approvals, in the United States and in foreign countries, may be
restricted or discontinued and the Company may fail to secure
</DIV>

<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt">32
</DIV>

<!-- PAGEBREAK -->
<P><HR noshade><P>
<H5 align="left" style="page-break-before:always"><A HREF="#tocpage">Table of Contents</A></H5><P>

<DIV style="font-family: 'Times New Roman',Times,serif">
<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">reimbursement or coverage for its products that are under development. The unavailability or
inadequacy of third-party reimbursement or coverage for <I>Niaspan</I>, <I>Advicor, Azmacort</I>, <I>Cardizem LA,
Teveten, </I>and <I>Teveten HCT </I>or the Company&#146;s products under development or those that may be acquired
or licensed, may reduce the demand for, or negatively affect the price of, these products, which
would have a material adverse effect on the Company.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The Company was recently informed by the Centers for Medicare and Medicaid Services of the
United States Department of Health and Human Services, referred to as CMS, that plans operating
under Part&nbsp;D (the New Medicare prescription drug program) may consider <I>Niaspan </I>to be a Part&nbsp;D drug
through May&nbsp;31, 2006, but may not consider <I>Niaspan </I>to be a Part&nbsp;D drug thereafter. CMS will not,
therefore, reimburse any Part&nbsp;D plan for <I>Niaspan </I>and standard Part&nbsp;D plans may not cover <I>Niaspan</I>,
after May&nbsp;31, 2006. CMS&#146; decision not to reimburse patients for <I>Niaspan </I>is based on its
determination that <I>Niaspan </I>is a &#147;prescription vitamin&#148;. Following initial discussions with CMS,
the Company sought FDA authorization to revise the <I>Niaspan </I>package insert and labeling language to
correctly define <I>Niaspan </I>as a therapeutic agent used in the treatment of dyslipidema and to clarify
that <I>Niaspan </I>is not a &#147;prescription vitamin&#148;. The FDA authorized the proposed labeling change for
immediate use with the revised wording and written confirmation of such acceptance by the FDA is
expected within the next few months. The Company has intensified its efforts to secure
reimbursement for <I>Niaspan </I>under Part&nbsp;D of the Medicare program but there is no guarantee that these
efforts will be successful. The failure to secure coverage for <I>Niaspan </I>under Part&nbsp;D of the
Medicare program could reduce the demand for <I>Niaspan </I>by patients with coverage under the Part&nbsp;D
program. Additionally, any decision by CMS to consider <I>Niaspan </I>a Part&nbsp;D drug does not guarantee
that any particular Part&nbsp;D plan will include <I>Niaspan </I>in its formulary. Further, if <I>Niaspan </I>were to
become a Part&nbsp;D drug, payment levels would depend on rebate contracts negotiated by the Company
with each Part&nbsp;D plan sponsor. To the extent that private insurers or managed care programs follow
Medicare coverage and payment developments, the adverse effects of the lack of Medicare coverage
and reimbursement developments may extend to private insurers adopting restricted coverage or lower
reimbursement policies, which could also result in reduced demand for <I>Niaspan</I>. A reduction in the
number of prescriptions written for <I>Niaspan </I>could have a material adverse effect on the Company&#146;s
business, operating results and financial condition.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B>Dependence on Collaborators</B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The Company relies on various third parties for certain aspects of the development,
manufacture and promotion of the Company&#146;s present and planned products. There can be no assurance
that the Company will be able to enter into future collaborative arrangements on favorable terms,
or at all. Even if the Company is successful in entering into such collaborative agreements, there
can be no assurance that any such arrangement will be successful. The success of any such
arrangement is dependent on, among other things, the skills, experience and efforts of the third
party&#146;s employees responsible for the project, the third party&#146;s commitment to the arrangement, and
the third party&#146;s financial condition, all of which are beyond the Company&#146;s control.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B>Dependence on Key Personnel</B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The Company&#146;s success is dependent on the Company&#146;s ability to attract and retain highly
qualified scientific, management, and sales personnel. In April&nbsp;2001, the Company hired its current
President and Chief Executive Officer, Adrian Adams. The loss of Mr.&nbsp;Adams as the Company&#146;s
President and Chief Executive Officer could have a material adverse effect on its operations and
business. Due to intense competition for personnel from other companies, academic institutions,
government entities, and other organizations, the Company may be unsuccessful in attracting and
retaining key personnel. The loss of key personnel, or the inability to attract and retain the
additional, highly-skilled employees required for the continuation and expansion of Kos&#146;
activities, could adversely affect its business and operating results.
</DIV>

<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt">33
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B>Products Under Development</B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Although the Company has obtained clearance from the FDA to market the products comprising its
Product Portfolio, the Company may be unsuccessful in effectively formulating any of its other
products as planned. Further, the Company may not be successful in demonstrating the safety and
efficacy of such products in human clinical trials. These trials may be costly and time-consuming.
The administration of any product the Company develops may produce undesirable side effects that
could result in the interruption, delay or suspension of clinical trials, or the failure to obtain
FDA or other regulatory approval for any or all targeted indications. Even if regulatory approval
is secured, the Company&#146;s products under development may later produce adverse effects that limit
or prevent their widespread use or that necessitate their withdrawal from the market. The Company
may discontinue the development of any of its products under development at any time.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B>Acquisition of Products</B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;As part of the Company&#146;s growth strategy, the Company intends to acquire and develop
additional product candidates or approved products. The success of this strategy depends upon Kos&#146;
ability to identify, select and acquire pharmaceutical products that meet the criteria it has
established. Any product candidate the Company acquires will require additional research and
development efforts prior to commercial sale, including extensive pre-clinical and/or clinical
testing and approval by the FDA and corresponding foreign regulatory authorities. All product
candidates are prone to the risks of failure inherent in pharmaceutical product development,
including the possibility that the product candidate will not be safe, non-toxic and effective or
approved by regulatory authorities. In addition, the Company cannot assure that any approved
products that it develops or acquires will be: (1)&nbsp;manufactured or produced economically; (2)
successfully commercialized; (3)&nbsp;widely accepted in the marketplace or that the Company will be
able to recover its significant expenditures in connection with the development or acquisition of
such products. In addition, proposing, negotiating and implementing an economically viable
acquisition is a lengthy and complex process. Other companies, including those with substantially
greater financial, marketing and sales resources, may compete with the Company for the acquisition
of product candidates and approved products. The Company may not be able to acquire the rights to
additional product candidates and approved products on terms that it finds acceptable, or at all.
In addition, if the Company acquires product candidates from third parties, the Company will be
dependent on third parties to supply such products to the Company for sale. The Company could be
materially adversely affected by the failure or inability of such suppliers to meet performance,
reliability and quality standards.
</DIV>

</DIV>
