<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B>Recent Developments</B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;On February&nbsp;22, 2006, the Company entered into a third Inventory Management Agreement (&#147;IMA&#148;)
with another of its three largest customers. Accordingly, the Company had signed IMAs with its three
largest customers as of such date.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;On February&nbsp;28, 2006, each of the Audit Committee and the Governance, Compliance and
Nominating Committee of the Board of Directors (collectively, the &#147;Committees&#148;) of the Company
approved limited and occasional use of a private jet fractionally-owned by Michael Jaharis, the
Company&#146;s Chairman Emeritus and principal shareholder, or an entity under his control, to transport
members of the Company&#146;s board of directors and senior management accompanying Mr.&nbsp;Jaharis to or
from Company-related meetings or for Company-related business.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The Committees determined that there may be instances when the Company would derive
substantial benefits as a result of having members of its board of directors and senior management
travel via private jet. The Committees considered a number of situations when commercial airline
travel may be impracticable or when the business interests of the Company would be served by having
members of management or the board travel together by private jet, as a result of time saved, more
productive use of time, confidentiality of discussions, and other circumstances when the business
interests of the Company would be served. For such occasions, the Committees authorized
reimbursement for a pro rata portion of the actual hourly costs to operate the jet multiplied by
the number of hours attributable to Company-related business, taking into account the number of
Company personnel and/or board members traveling on board with Mr.&nbsp;Jaharis, and provided that
reimbursement will in no event exceed the actual cost for use and operation of the jet. The
Committees will monitor the Company&#146;s use of and reimbursement for the jet on an ongoing basis to
ensure such use and reimbursement is reasonable and consistent with the Company&#146;s policies and
practices.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B>Results of Operations</B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt"><I>Critical Accounting Policies</I>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The Company&#146;s significant accounting policies are described in Note 2 to the Consolidated
Financial Statements included in this Form 10-K . The Company believes that its most
critical accounting policies include revenue recognition, the estimation of allowances principally
related to product returns and discounts, managed care rebates, chargebacks, issues related to
price reporting to the government under various federal programs (including the Medicaid program),
accounting for income taxes, and management&#146;s estimate of the useful lives and
realizability of recorded intangible assets.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><U>Revenue Recognition</U>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The Company&#146;s total revenues include net sales and licensing revenue. Revenues from net sales
and the related cost of goods sold are recognized at the time that product is accepted by the
Company&#146;s customers. Revenue and the related cost of goods sold are not recognized if the Company
believes that there is excess inventory in the distribution channel, taking into account, among
other things, product
</DIV>

<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt">55
</DIV>

<!-- PAGEBREAK -->
<P><HR noshade><P>
<H5 align="left" style="page-break-before:always"><A HREF="#tocpage">Table of Contents</A></H5><P>

<DIV style="font-family: 'Times New Roman',Times,serif">
<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">return history, prescription data and data obtained through the Company&#146;s IMAs (see IMA
discussion below).
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The Company recognizes licensing revenue when the obligations associated with the earning of
such revenue have been satisfied, based upon the terms of the contract. If obligations associated
with the earning of such revenue remain, the Company defers all or a portion of the payment,
whether or not it is refundable, and recognizes such amount over future periods after the remaining
services have been rendered or delivery has occurred and the amounts
are fixed or determinable. The Company&#146;s policy related to licensing revenue resulting from milestone and upfront
payments under the Merck Agreement is to recognize such licensing revenue using a
proportional-performance revenue recognition method.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Price reporting to the government under the Medicaid program bears directly on the calculation
and payment of Medicaid rebates, which the Company may owe. The Company records accrual estimates
for sales returns and allowances mostly based on historical experience. The calculation of rebates
and chargebacks is based on existing contractual arrangements with indirect customers (such as
managed care providers, pharmacy benefit administrators, and government units) and on Kos&#146; analysis
of estimated product inventory levels in its distribution channel, which is derived through the use
of certain inputs. The Company believes that its estimation of sales allowances related to product
returns and discounts, managed care rebates and chargebacks represent the best estimates of those
amounts, and are based on assumptions which the Company believes represent the most likely
outcomes. The Company has discovered an error in its calculation of Best Price (&#147;Best Price&#148;), has
informed the government of that error, and has retained the necessary external expertise to
thoroughly evaluate the methodologies used to report prices to the various applicable government
programs. This methodology review could lead to the identification of additional errors or other
changes that might increase the Company&#146;s rebate or other financial obligations under the
government programs in which it or its products participate.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The most pertinent inputs used in the estimation of the Company sales allowances and accruals,
and product inventory levels in its distribution channel (as discussed below), include prescription
data (derived from a third party publication), consumer price index (derived from a third party
publication), product Best Price (as calculated by the Company based on its available data), and
Average Manufacturer Price (&#147;AMP&#148;) (as calculated by the Company based on its available data). Of
these inputs, anticipated prescription data and AMP require significant estimation and/or the
application of methodologies in order to ensure that they meet various accounting and price
reporting requirements. The following table reflects the potential impact to revenue for the year
ended December&nbsp;31, 2005, based upon various combinations of these reasonably likely outcomes in
estimated prescription data and AMP (positive dollar amounts represent potential decreases in
revenue; negative dollar amounts represent potential increases in revenue):
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The Company&#146;s management periodically reviews the policies and estimates discussed above, the
effect of which is reflected as a component of net income in the period in which a change is
known. Other than the changes described below associated with the Company&#146;s estimate of
its product return exposure during the first quarter of 2003, the adjustment to the deferred tax
asset valuation allowance as a result of the change in judgment about the realizability of such
asset, and the change in inventory level estimate during the fourth quarter of 2005, such changes
to these estimates have not been material to the Company&#146;s results of operations during the years
ended December&nbsp;31, 2005, 2004 and 2003.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The Company periodically evaluates the volume of its <I>Niaspan, Advicor, Azmacort, Cardizem LA,
Teveten </I>and <I>Teveten HCT </I>products that are in customer inventories or elsewhere in the distribution
channel to determine whether increased risk of product returns exists. For the period from
the introduction of its internally-developed products through December&nbsp;31, 2002, Kos&#146; return risk
expectations were consistently based on its limited product return experience given the early stage
nature of its products and of the Company. Accordingly, Kos established a specific return
risk estimate based on estimated inventory levels in the distribution channel (see further
discussion of inventory levels below) that was used to determine the amount of revenue that could
be recorded during a given period. During the quarter ended March&nbsp;31, 2003, as a result of
the significant history of minimal returns for the <I>Niaspan </I>and <I>Advicor </I>products since their
introduction, Kos revised its return risk estimates to reflect the historically low product return
patterns. This change in accounting estimate resulted in the Company recognizing as
revenue all product shipments made during the quarter ended March&nbsp;31, 2003, as well as $11.1
million of prior period product shipments not recognized as revenue because of Kos&#146; previous
product return risk exposure estimates. The impact of this change in estimate increased
the Company&#146;s reported revenues, net income, and basic and diluted earnings per share by $11.1
million, $9.9&nbsp;million, and $0.45 per share and $0.24 per share, respectively, for the year ended
December&nbsp;31, 2003. The Company will continue to monitor wholesaler inventory levels, and,
if the Company&#146;s product return risk exceeds acceptable levels, the Company may be required to not
recognize the revenue and related costs associated with the excess inventory until such return risk
is mitigated.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;During the fourth quarter of 2005 and first quarter of 2006, the Company signed IMAs with its
three largest customers. These agreements have enhanced the Company&#146;s ability to monitor inventory
levels for marketed products and, as a result, the Company amended its estimates for inventory
levels in the distribution channel as of December&nbsp;31, 2005. Prior to signing its IMAs, the Company
estimated inventory levels using net sales and estimated withdrawals (using prescription data) from
the marketplace. The impact of this change in estimate resulted in an increase in net sales, net
income and basic and diluted
</DIV>

<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt">57
</DIV>

<DIV style="font-family: 'Times New Roman',Times,serif">
<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">earnings per share for the year ended December&nbsp;31, 2005 of $17.0&nbsp;million, $7.9&nbsp;million, and
$0.18 per share and $0.17 per share, respectively. The Company believes that the IMAs provide for a
more precise and accurate estimate of product inventory in its distribution channel by allowing
direct access to product inventory held by its top three customers, which accounted for
approximately 81% of total revenue in 2005. The Company further believes that IMAs may result in a
reduction of inventory in the Company&#146;s distribution channels during the year ended December&nbsp;31,
2006, equivalent to approximately $45&nbsp;million in net sales.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><U>Income Taxes</U>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The Company follows Statement of Financial Accounting Standards No.&nbsp;109, &#147;Accounting for
Income Taxes&#148; (&#147;SFAS 109&#148;), which requires, among other things, recognition of future tax benefits
and liabilities measured at enacted tax rates attributable to temporary differences between
financial statement and income tax bases of assets and liabilities and to tax net operating loss
carryforwards to the extent that realization of said benefits is more likely than not.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Through December&nbsp;31, 2003, the Company had established a valuation allowance against its net
deferred tax assets because there was not sufficient evidence to conclude that the Company would
&#147;more likely than not&#148; realize all or a portion of such assets. Management currently believes,
based on the Company&#146;s historical profitability and on its expected future profitability, that the
Company will generate sufficient taxable income to realize its deferred tax assets prior to the
expiration of any net operating loss carryforwards (&#147;NOLs&#148;) and, therefore, that the Company will
&#147;more likely than not&#148; realize most of its deferred tax assets.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Under SFAS 109, the valuation allowance should be adjusted when a change in circumstances
causes a change in judgment about the realizability of deferred tax assets. The portion of the
valuation allowance related to NOLs expected to be utilized to offset estimated &#147;ordinary&#148; income
in the current year is included in the computation of the estimated annual effective tax rate. The
portion of the valuation allowance related to other deferred tax assets, including net operating
losses expected to be utilized to offset ordinary income in future years, is reversed as of the
date of the change in circumstances. Accordingly, the benefit from income taxes in the accompanying
condensed consolidated statement of operations for the year ended December&nbsp;31, 2004, includes the
reversal of $71.2&nbsp;million of valuation allowance, of which $67.3&nbsp;million represents the portion
reversed through the effective tax rate for the period and $3.9&nbsp;million relates to the reversal of
valuation allowance on deferred tax assets expected to be realized through ordinary income in
future years. The benefit for income taxes also includes a $14.4&nbsp;million deferred benefit related
to the <I>Azmacort </I>In-process R&#038;D Write-off. At December&nbsp;31, 2004, the Company had a remaining
valuation allowance of approximately $4.2&nbsp;million. For the year ended December&nbsp;31, 2005, the $70.5
million provision for income taxes in the accompanying condensed consolidated statement of
operations includes the reversal of $0.4&nbsp;million of valuation allowance.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Included in the Company&#146;s $54.5&nbsp;million of net deferred tax assets as of December&nbsp;31, 2004,
were $12.5&nbsp;million of federal NOLs available to offset future federal taxable income and $152.9
million of state NOLs available to offset future state taxable income. In addition, the Company had
$4.7&nbsp;million of other tax credits to offset future federal income tax. As of December&nbsp;31, 2005, the
Company had approximately $85.2&nbsp;million of state net operating losses available to offset future
taxable income. These NOLs will expire between 2006 and 2022. Deferred tax assets, net, as of
December&nbsp;31, 2005, totaled approximately $66.1&nbsp;million. If Kos is unable to generate sufficient
future taxable income through operating results, or if its estimates about future profitability
change significantly, a portion or all of its deferred tax assets may have to be reserved through
adjustments to the provision for income taxes.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;As of December&nbsp;31, 2005, the Company had other liabilities of $7.9&nbsp;million related to
uncertain tax positions. The Company&#146;s liabilities for uncertain tax positions are analyzed
periodically and adjustments are made as events occur to warrant such adjustment.
</DIV>

</DIV>

<H5 align="left" style="page-break-before:always"><A HREF="#tocpage">Table of Contents</A></H5><P>

<DIV style="font-family: 'Times New Roman',Times,serif">

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><U>Intangible Assets</U>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;In accordance with SFAS No.&nbsp;144, &#147;Accounting for the Impairment or Disposal of Long-Lived
Assets&#148;, the Company periodically evaluates whether current facts or circumstances indicate that
the carrying value of its depreciable long-lived assets to be held and used may not be recoverable.
If such circumstances are determined to exist, an estimate of undiscounted future cash flows
produced by the long-lived asset, or the appropriate grouping of assets, is compared to the
carrying value to determine whether an impairment exists. If an asset is determined to be impaired,
the loss is measured based on the difference between the asset&#146;s fair value and its carrying value.
An estimate of the asset&#146;s fair value is based on quoted market prices in active markets, if
available. If quoted market prices are not available, the estimate of fair value is based on
various valuation techniques, including a discounted value of estimated future cash flows. The
Company reports an asset to be disposed of at the lower of its carrying value or its estimated net
realizable value.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Goodwill is evaluated at least annually for impairment in accordance with SFAS No.&nbsp;142,
&#147;Goodwill and Other Intangible Assets&#148; (&#147;SFAS 142&#148;). SFAS 142 requires that goodwill be tested for
impairment using a two-step process. The first step is to identify a potential impairment, and the
second step measures the amount of the impairment loss, if any. Goodwill is deemed to be impaired
if the carrying amount of a reporting unit&#146;s goodwill exceeds its estimated fair value. SFAS 142
requires that indefinite-lived intangible assets be tested for impairment using a one-step process,
which consists of a comparison of the fair value to the carrying value of the intangible asset.
Such intangible assets are deemed to be impaired if their net book value exceeds their estimated
fair value.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The Company did not recognize any impairment losses related to its intangible assets or
goodwill during the years ended December&nbsp;31, 2005, 2004 and 2003.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The estimates of future cash flows, based on reasonable and supportable assumptions and
projections, require management&#146;s judgment. Any changes in key assumptions about the Company&#146;s
businesses and their prospects, or changes in market conditions, could result in an impairment
charge.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The increase in revenue was principally attributable to increases in unit volume and price for
the Company&#146;s <I>Niaspan </I>and <I>Advicor </I>products during 2005 as compared to 2004, the full year impact of
the
</DIV>

<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt">59
</DIV>

<!-- PAGEBREAK -->
<P><HR noshade><P>
<H5 align="left" style="page-break-before:always"><A HREF="#tocpage">Table of Contents</A></H5><P>

<DIV style="font-family: 'Times New Roman',Times,serif">
<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">commercialization of the <I>Azmacort </I>product, which began in April&nbsp;2004, as well as the Kos
commercialization of <I>Cardizem LA </I>and <I>Teveten </I>Products, which began in May&nbsp;2005. Of the $123.9
million increase in <I>Niaspan </I>and <I>Advicor </I>net sales during 2005, $50.8&nbsp;million resulted from
increases in unit volume and $73.1&nbsp;million resulted from increases in price. The Company believes
that in 2005, the ratio of unit volume growth relative to pricing growth was negatively impacted by
the reduction in inventory levels that resulted from the IMA agreements.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The Company&#146;s revenue for the year ended December&nbsp;31, 2005, was also affected by an accounting
change for licensing revenue relative to its international commercialization agreement (the &#147;Merck
Agreement&#148;) with Merck KGaA. The Company&#146;s policy with respect to recording licensing revenue
resulting from Merck KGaA product sales is to record such licensing revenue as the related product
sales are generated by Merck KGaA. Through March&nbsp;31, 2005, the Company&#146;s policy related to
licensing revenue resulting from milestone and upfront payments was to recognize such licensing
revenue over the then remaining agreement period using the straight-line method and to recognize
reimbursement payments as an offset to operating expenses over the then remaining agreement period
using the straight-line method. During the quarter ended June&nbsp;30, 2005, the Company re-evaluated
its policy for recognizing such licensing revenue and reimbursement payments and determined that a
proportional-performance revenue recognition method more appropriately reflected Kos&#146; continuing
obligations relative to the arrangement with Merck KGaA, while also incorporating the recognition
of revenue relative to the efforts extended up to the point of receiving the upfront and
reimbursement payments and attaining marketing authorization (the &#147;Merck Accounting Change&#148;).
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The Merck Accounting Change represents a correction of an accounting error by the Company. The
Merck Accounting Change increased the Company&#146;s revenue and pre-tax income by $1.8&nbsp;million, or
$0.04 per fully diluted share, for the year ended December&nbsp;31, 2005, as well as increased net
income by $1.1&nbsp;million, or $0.02 per fully diluted share, for the year ended December&nbsp;31, 2005.
Including the effect of the Merck Accounting Change, the Company recorded $3.3&nbsp;million of licensing
revenue associated with the amortization of upfront and milestone payments received pursuant to the
Merck Agreement during the year ended December&nbsp;31, 2005, as compared to $1.1&nbsp;million in 2004.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;As more fully described above, the Company records provisions for the estimation of allowances
principally related to managed care rebates, chargebacks related to Medicaid and Medicare, product
returns and discounts, and IMA fees as components of revenues. An analysis of the Company&#146;s gross
sales, by product, subject to each of these provisions for the years ended December&nbsp;31, 2005 and
2004, follows:
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The Company had accrual balances related to its managed care rebates, Medicaid rebate
obligations, chargebacks and IMA fees of $79.3&nbsp;million as of December&nbsp;31, 2005, and accrual
balances related to its managed care rebates, Medicaid rebate obligations and chargebacks of $50.0
million as of December&nbsp;31, 2004. Furthermore, the Company had allowances against its trade
accounts receivable for product returns and discounts of $3.5&nbsp;million and $2.8&nbsp;million, as of
December&nbsp;31, 2005 and 2004, respectively.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Cost of sales increased 120.2% to $81.3&nbsp;million for the year ended December&nbsp;31, 2005, from
$36.9&nbsp;million in 2004, primarily as a result of increased unit sales of <I>Niaspan, </I>the addition of
<I>Azmacort </I>to the Company&#146;s product portfolio on March&nbsp;31, 2004, and the addition of <I>Cardizem LA </I>and
the <I>Teveten </I>Products to the Company&#146;s product portfolio during the second quarter 2005. In 2005,
cost of sales was approximately 10.9% of net sales, as compared to 7.5% in 2004. The increase was
primarily a result of changes in the product mix, including the impact of the addition of <I>Azmacort,
Cardizem LA, </I>and the <I>Teveten </I>Products to the Company&#146;s product offering, partially offset by price
increases as described above.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The Company&#146;s research and development expenses increased 10.9% to $123.2&nbsp;million for the year
ended December&nbsp;31, 2005, from $111.1&nbsp;million in 2004. The increased expenses related
primarily to the Jerini Licensing Payment of $14.2&nbsp;million, the Arisaph Write-off of $4.0&nbsp;million,
the <I>Cardizem LA </I>Combination Product In-process R&#038;D Write-off of $2.8&nbsp;million and to increases of
$11.1&nbsp;million principally associated with clinical studies for the Company&#146;s products under
development, of $7.5&nbsp;million in personnel and personnel-related costs, of $5.4&nbsp;million in
formulation costs for the Company&#146;s products under development, and of $4.8&nbsp;million in medical
education programs. These increases were partially offset by the absence, during the 2005 period,
of the $38.0&nbsp;million charge related to the <I>Azmacort </I>In-process R&#038;D Write-off recorded during the
year ended December&nbsp;31, 2004.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Selling, general and administrative expenses increased 55.3% to $366.1&nbsp;million for the year
ended December&nbsp;31, 2005, from $235.7&nbsp;million in 2004. Within this category, selling
expenses increased to $305.2&nbsp;million in 2005, from $197.2&nbsp;million in 2004. The growth in
selling expenses was primarily related to increases of $64.1&nbsp;million in royalty expenses, of $28.0
million in sales force operating costs in support of the Company&#146;s products, of $9.7&nbsp;million
related to marketing efforts to promote the Company&#146;s products, and of $4.9&nbsp;million of amortization
related to the <I>Azmacort </I>purchase. General and administrative expenses increased to $60.9&nbsp;million
for the year ended December&nbsp;31, 2005, from $38.5&nbsp;million in 2004. This increase in general
and administrative expenses was primarily related to increases of $4.8&nbsp;million in professional
fees, of $4.2&nbsp;million in personnel and personnel related costs (which included a $1.1&nbsp;million
charge related to the modification of a former employee&#146;s stock options), of a $3.9&nbsp;million charge
related to consultant stock options, to the absence, during 2005, of the one-time $6.0&nbsp;million
trademark settlement, of which $2.0&nbsp;million representing a reimbursement of legal costs was
recorded as an offset to general and administrative expenses in 2004 and of $7.5&nbsp;million in other
costs associated with the expanded activities of the Company.
</DIV>

<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt">61
</DIV>

<!-- PAGEBREAK -->
<P><HR noshade><P>
<H5 align="left" style="page-break-before:always"><A HREF="#tocpage">Table of Contents</A></H5><P>

<DIV style="font-family: 'Times New Roman',Times,serif">

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Other income increased 61.7% to $7.6&nbsp;million for the year ended December&nbsp;31, 2005, from $4.7
million in 2004. The increase in other income included a $6.0&nbsp;million increase in interest income
resulting from higher balances of cash and cash equivalents outstanding during 2005, as compared to
2004. The increase in interest income was partially offset by the effect of a $6&nbsp;million settlement
received from Andrx in 2004, of which $4.0&nbsp;million was recorded as other income, and by a decrease
of $0.7&nbsp;million in interest expense under the Company&#146;s credit facilities. The decrease in interest
expense is mostly attributable to a decrease in outstanding borrowings.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Excluding the benefit resulting from the $0.4&nbsp;million and $71.2&nbsp;million reversals of valuation
allowance (as discussed above), the Company recorded a provision for income taxes of $70.9&nbsp;million
and $47.0&nbsp;million for the years ended December&nbsp;31, 2005 and 2004, respectively. The $23.9&nbsp;million
increase in provision for income taxes was primarily a result of increased taxable income and of
$7.9&nbsp;million in accruals for potential tax liabilities during 2005, offset by a $5.4&nbsp;million tax
benefit recorded during 2005 for certain cumulative research and development activities of the
Company.
</DIV>





<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The Company&#146;s net income decreased 17.0% to $118.1&nbsp;million for the year ended December&nbsp;31,
2005, compared with $142.3&nbsp;million for the year ended December&nbsp;31, 2004. The decrease in net income
was primarily a result of an increase in income before taxes, offset by the absence, in 2005, of a
reversal of the Company&#146;s deferred tax asset valuation allowance, as described above.
</DIV>

Of the $92.6&nbsp;million increase in <I>Niaspan </I>net sales during 2004, approximately $52.8&nbsp;million
resulted from
</DIV>

<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt">62
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">increases in unit volume and approximately $39.8&nbsp;million resulted from increases in

Additionally, the 2004 period includes revenue for the <I>Azmacort </I>product, for which
commercialization began on April&nbsp;1, 2004. These increases were partially offset by the change in
product return estimates (as described above), which increased 2003 revenue by $11.1&nbsp;million.

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The Company had accrual balances related to its managed care rebates, Medicaid rebate
obligations and chargebacks of $50.0&nbsp;million and $18.2&nbsp;million, as of December&nbsp;31, 2004 and 2003,
respectively. Furthermore, the Company had allowances against its trade accounts receivable for
product returns and discounts of $2.8&nbsp;million and $2.1&nbsp;million, as of December&nbsp;31, 2004 and 2003,
respectively.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Cost of sales increased 84% to $36.9&nbsp;million for the year ended December&nbsp;31, 2004, from $20.0
million for the same period in 2003, primarily as a result of increased unit sales of the <I>Niaspan</I>
and <I>Advicor </I>products, combined with the addition of the <I>Azmacort </I>product to the Company&#146;s product
portfolio on March&nbsp;31, 2004. In 2004, cost of sales was approximately 7.5% of net sales, as
compared to 6.8% for the same period in 2003. The increase was primarily a result of changes in the
product mix, including the impact of the addition of <I>Azmacort </I>to the Company&#146;s product offering,
partially offset by price increases as described above.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The Company&#146;s research and development expenses increased 113% to $111.1&nbsp;million for the year
ended December&nbsp;31, 2004, from $52.2&nbsp;million for the same period in 2003 . The increased
expenses related primarily to a one-time charge of $38.0&nbsp;million related to the <I>Azmacort </I>In-process
R&#038;D Write-off, and to increases of $7.9&nbsp;million principally associated with clinical studies for
the Company&#146;s products under development, of $7.4&nbsp;million in personnel and personnel-related costs,
and of $1.9&nbsp;million in medical educational programs.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Selling, general and administrative expenses increased 51% to $235.7&nbsp;million for the year
ended December&nbsp;31, 2004, from $156.5&nbsp;million for the same period in 2003 . Within this
category, selling expenses increased to $197.2&nbsp;million for the 2004 period from $124.3&nbsp;million for
the comparable 2003 period . The growth in selling expenses was primarily related to
increases of $38.5&nbsp;million in royalty expenses, of $20.6&nbsp;million in sales force operating costs in
support of the <I>Niaspan, Advicor, </I>and <I>Azmacort </I>products and of $10.4&nbsp;million of amortization related
to the <I>Azmacort </I>purchase. General and administrative expenses increased to $38.5&nbsp;million for the
year ended December&nbsp;31, 2004, from $32.2&nbsp;million for the year ended December&nbsp;31, 2003 .
This increase in general and administrative expenses was primarily related to increases of
$3.0&nbsp;million in personnel and personnel related costs, of $1.3&nbsp;million in professional fees, and of
$4.0&nbsp;million in other costs associated with the expanded activities of the
</DIV>

</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;As previously described, the benefit for income taxes in the accompanying consolidated

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Interest expense under the Company&#146;s credit facilities (see discussion below) totaled $1.2

in interest expense is mostly attributable to a decrease in outstanding borrowings.

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The Company&#146;s net income increased 140% to $142.3&nbsp;million for the year ended December&nbsp;31,
2004, compared with $59.4&nbsp;million for the year ended December&nbsp;31, 2003.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B>Liquidity and Capital Resources</B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;At December&nbsp;31, 2005, the Company had cash and cash equivalents of $412.7&nbsp;million and working
capital of $392.2&nbsp;million. The Company&#146;s primary uses of cash to date have been to fund selling,
general and administrative expenses, research and development expenses, and strategic investments
in product marketing and licensing rights.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Net cash provided by operating activities was $237.5&nbsp;million in 2005, compared to $196.4
million in 2004, and $55.6&nbsp;million in 2003. The increase in net cash provided by operating
activities in 2005 was primarily a result of the increase in net income adjusted for non-cash
items, as well as increases in working capital sources of cash. Working capital sources of
cash during 2005 included increases in accounts payable, accrued
expenses and other liabilities as well as decreases in deferred tax
assets and other assets,
partially offset by increases in trade accounts receivable, inventories, prepaid expenses and other
current assets. The net cash provided by operating activities in
2004 was primarily a result of net income adjusted for non-cash items as well as increases in
working capital sources of cash. Working capital sources of cash during 2004 included decreases in
inventory as well as increases in accounts payable, accrued expenses and advance payments received
on license agreements, partially offset by increases in trade accounts receivable, prepaid expenses
and other current assets, and deferred tax assets. The net cash provided by operating
activities in 2003 was primarily a result of net income adjusted for non-cash items partially
offset by working capital uses of cash. Working capital uses of cash in 2003 included increases in
trade accounts receivable, inventories, prepaid expenses and other current assets, and decreases in
advance payments from customers, partially offset by increases in accounts payable, accrued
expenses and advance payments received on license agreements.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Net cash used in investing activities was $139.4&nbsp;million in 2005, compared to $215.6&nbsp;million
in 2004, and $11.0&nbsp;million in 2003. The decrease in cash used in investing activities
related principally to the absence, during the 2005 period, of the $206.1&nbsp;million purchase of the
<I>Azmacort </I>product, completed in 2004, which was partially offset by the $110.0&nbsp;million purchase of
intangible assets, principally related to the Biovail Agreements, the $11.8&nbsp;million Jerini
Investment, and the $4.0&nbsp;million investment in Arisaph in 2005. The increase in cash used in
investing activities in 2004, compared to 2003, was principally related to the absence, during the
2003 period, of the <I>Azmacort </I>purchase described above.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;In 2005, net cash provided by financing activities was $55.9&nbsp;million, compared to $18.0
million in 2004, and $195.8&nbsp;million in 2003. The increase in net cash provided by financing
activities in 2005 was primarily related to an increase in cash received from the exercise of stock
options and from employee participation in the employee stock purchase plan during 2005. The
decrease in net cash provided by

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;On December&nbsp;19, 2002, the Company entered into an agreement with Michael Jaharis, Chairman
Emeritus of the Company&#146;s Board of Directors and its principal shareholder, whereby Mr.&nbsp;Jaharis
agreed to replace the previous $30-million credit facility extended to the Company on July&nbsp;1, 1998
(which was to expire on December&nbsp;31, 2002), with another facility expiring on June&nbsp;30, 2008 (the
&#147;Additional Standby Facility&#148;). In connection with this new credit arrangement, the Company
granted to Mr.&nbsp;Jaharis non-detachable warrants to purchase 1,000,000 shares of the Company&#146;s Common
Stock at an exercise price based on the market price of the Company&#146;s Common Stock on the date that
the first draw under this facility occurs. The Company had no borrowings outstanding under the
Additional Standby Facility as of December&nbsp;31, 2005. Borrowings, if and when outstanding, will bear
interest at the prime rate (7.25% as of December&nbsp;31, 2005), and will be subject to standard and
customary loan covenants, as well as the conditions that the death of the lender shall not have
occurred, that lender, his spouse, children and entities they control continue to own at least 40%
of the Common Stock of the Company, and that no material adverse change shall have occurred to the
Company or its financial operations. On January&nbsp;10, 2005, Mr.&nbsp;Jaharis transferred all of his
existing rights and obligations under the Additional Standby Facility to his wife, Mary Jaharis.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;On September&nbsp;1, 1999, the Company formally agreed to the terms of an additional $50-million
Supplemental Credit Facility initially entered into with Mr.&nbsp;Jaharis on October&nbsp;7, 1998.
On July&nbsp;21, 2001, the Company replaced its existing $50&nbsp;million promissory note payable to Mr.
Jaharis with two, $25&nbsp;million, promissory notes, one payable in the name of Michael Jaharis and the
other payable in the name of Mary Jaharis. With this promissory note replacement, all of
Mr.&nbsp;Jaharis&#146; existing rights and obligations under the Supplemental Credit Facility, with respect
to one-half of the outstanding amount, were transferred to Mrs.&nbsp;Jaharis, and were subsequently
transferred by Mrs.&nbsp;Jaharis to a limited partnership (the &#147;Limited Partnership&#148;) that she
controlled. All other terms and conditions of the Supplemental Credit Facility remained
unchanged. Borrowings under the Supplemental Credit Facility were convertible at $4.91 per
share and accrued interest at the prime rate. On November&nbsp;25, 2003, in connection with an equity
offering of the Company&#146;s Common Stock pursuant to an effective shelf registration statement, the
Limited Partnership exercised its right to convert $6,137,500 of borrowings outstanding under the
Supplemental Credit Facility into 1,250,000 shares of the Company&#146;s Common Stock. Those shares were
then sold by the Limited Partnership as part of the equity offering. The Company did not receive
any proceeds from such sale by the Limited Partnership. On December&nbsp;31, 2003, all then outstanding
borrowings under the Supplemental Credit Facility, which totaled $43,862,500 and bore interest at
the prime rate, were converted into 8,933,299 shares of the Company&#146;s Common Stock. The
Supplemental Credit Facility terminated as of December&nbsp;31, 2003.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;On December&nbsp;21, 1999, Mr.&nbsp;Jaharis extended a $50-million loan to the Company (the &#147;Standby
Facility&#148;) which matured on June&nbsp;30, 2005. In lieu of a conversion feature, the Company
granted to Mr.&nbsp;Jaharis non-detachable warrants to purchase 6,000,000 shares of the Company&#146;s Common
Stock at $5.00 per share, which approximated the market value of the Company&#146;s Common Stock on the
effective date of the Standby Facility. Mr.&nbsp;Jaharis exercised 2,000,000 and 200,000
warrants to purchase shares of the Company&#146;s Common Stock at $5.00 per share on October&nbsp;13, 2004,
and November&nbsp;24, 2004, respectively. On January&nbsp;10, 2005, Mr.&nbsp;Jaharis transferred all of his
existing rights and obligations under the Standby Facility to his wife, Mary Jaharis. In June&nbsp;2005,
Mrs.&nbsp;Jaharis exercised the remaining 3,800,000 warrants to purchase shares of the Company&#146;s Common
Stock under the Standby Facility, thereby relieving the Company of $19&nbsp;million of debt outstanding.
As of June&nbsp;30, 2005, the Standby Facility was terminated.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;In January&nbsp;2002, the Securities and Exchange Commission declared effective a shelf
registration statement (the &#147;Shelf Registration&#148;) filed by the Company for the sale, from time to
time, of up to $200&nbsp;million of its Common Stock, Preferred Stock, stock options, warrants and other
rights to purchase Common Stock or Preferred Stock. On October&nbsp;10, 2003, the Company filed
a new shelf registration statement that covered the shares of the earlier Shelf Registration (the
&#147;Amended Shelf Registration&#148;) and which allowed the Limited Partnership to sell up to 1,500,000
shares of the Company&#146;s Common Stock in a public offering; and on October&nbsp;31, 2003, the Company
further amended the Amended Shelf Registration to register an aggregate of 1,350,000 shares of
Company Common Stock held by Bristol-Myers Squibb Company and the Company&#146;s Chairman. The
Amended Shelf Registration was declared effective by the Securities and Exchange Commission on
October&nbsp;31, 2003. On November&nbsp;25, 2003, the Company sold 3,750,000 shares of Common Stock
and the Limited Partnership sold 1,250,000 shares of Common Stock (converted from the Supplemental
Credit Facility) pursuant to the Amended Shelf Registration. On December&nbsp;22, 2003, the
Company sold an additional 650,000 shares of Common Stock and the Company&#146;s Chairman sold 100,000
shares of Common Stock to cover over-allotments as permitted in the Amended Shelf Registration. Net
proceeds to the Company resulting from the sale of the 4,400,000 shares of Common Stock totaled
$184.3&nbsp;million. Proceeds from the offerings, other than the proceeds pertaining to the selling
shareholders, were mostly used to finance the purchase of the <I>Azmacort </I>product from Aventis.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Although the Company currently anticipates that, including the capital available to the
Company under the Additional Standby Facility, it has, or has access to, an amount of working
capital that will be sufficient to fund the Company&#146;s operations for the next twelve months, the
Company&#146;s cash requirements during this period will be substantial and may exceed the amount of
working capital available to the Company. The Company&#146;s ability to fund its operating
requirements and maintain an adequate level of working capital will depend primarily on its ability
to continue to generate substantial growth in sales of its <I>Niaspan</I>, <I>Advicor</I>, <I>Azmacort</I>, <I>Cardizem LA</I>,
<I>Teveten </I>and <I>Teveten HCT </I>products, its ability to continue to access its remaining credit facility,
and on its ability to control operating expenses. The Company&#146;s failure to generate
substantial growth in the sales of <I>Niaspan</I>, <I>Advicor</I>, <I>Azmacort</I>, <I>Cardizem LA</I>, <I>Teveten </I>and <I>Teveten HCT</I>
products, control operating expenses, or meet the conditions necessary for the Company to obtain
funding under the Additional Standby Facility, and other events &#151; including the progress of the
Company&#146;s research, development and clinical trial programs; the costs and timing of seeking
regulatory approvals of the Company&#146;s products under development; the Company&#146;s ability to obtain
regulatory approvals in the United States and abroad; the Company&#146;s ability to maintain its
compliance with FDA regulations and standards without adversely affecting its manufacturing
capability or ability to meet its production requirements or profit margins; the Company&#146;s ability
to manufacture or have manufactured products at an economically feasible cost and in sufficient
quantities; costs associated with filing, prosecuting, defending, and enforcing patent claims and
other intellectual property rights; the extent and terms of any collaborative research,
manufacturing, marketing, joint venture, or other arrangements; the Company&#146;s ability to maintain
coverage and reimbursement of its products by government agencies and the effects of the loss of
such coverage and reimbursement with such agencies, such as the Centers for Medicare and Medicaid
Services; reduction of inventory levels in the Company&#146;s distribution channels as a result of the
IMAs; the Company&#146;s ability to ensure compliance with prescription drug sales and marketing laws
and regulations; and changes in economic, regulatory, or competitive conditions or the Company&#146;s
planned business &#151; could cause the Company to require additional capital. In the event
that the Company must raise additional capital to fund its working capital needs, it may seek to
raise such capital through loans, the issuance of debt securities or equity securities, each of
which would require consent under the Additional Standby Facility . To the extent the
Company raises additional capital by issuing equity securities or obtaining borrowings convertible
into equity,

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;As of December&nbsp;31, 2005, the only long-term obligations of the Company subject to interest
expense were its capital leases. The Company will be subject to interest expense related to its
capital leases of approximately $7,000 during the period from 2006 through 2007.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Purchase orders or contracts for the purchase of raw materials and other goods and services
are not included in the table above . The Company&#146;s purchase orders are based on its
current manufacturing or operating needs and are fulfilled by its vendors within a short period of
time. As of December&nbsp;31, 2005, the Company had commitments for the purchase of raw materials and
finished goods inventory with its vendors totaling approximately $2.0&nbsp;million and $27.6&nbsp;million,
respectively.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Contractual obligations such as sponsored research and licensing agreements that are
contingent upon the achievement of certain milestones are not included in the table above. Such
arrangements are not considered contractual obligations until the milestone is met by the third
party and, in most cases, are cancelable at the option of the Company . As of December&nbsp;31,
2005, assuming all future milestones were met, additional required payments related to sponsored
research and licensing agreements, including a sponsored research agreement with Arisaph, would be
approximately $2.6&nbsp;million. Additionally, the Company is obligated, under an agreement with
Arisaph, to invest an additional $4&nbsp;million in Arisaph if certain milestones are met by Arisaph by
August&nbsp;2006.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;On October&nbsp;23, 2002, the Company signed the Merck Agreement for the marketing of <I>Niaspan </I>and
<I>Advicor </I>products outside the United States, Canada and Japan. As of December&nbsp;31, 2005, in
connection with the Merck Agreement, Kos had received $20.0&nbsp;million in upfront, reimbursement, and
milestone payments from Merck KGaA, including $15.0&nbsp;million of upfront and reimbursement payments
(of which $3.8&nbsp;million is currently refundable to Merck KGaA if Kos fails to achieve certain
regulatory milestones). Refundable amounts under the Merck Agreement are not included in the table
above.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;On November&nbsp;4, 2003, the Company and Takeda announced a three-year agreement to co-promote
<I>Niaspan </I>and <I>Advicor </I>in the United States. On April&nbsp;12, 2005, the Company entered into the Duramed
Co-promotion Agreement to co-promote <I>Niaspan </I>and <I>Advicor </I>in the United States. Obligations
associated with Takeda, Duramed and with a contract sales organization (&#147;CSO&#148;) are based upon net
sales of the Company and not included in the table above. The Company pays Duramed royalties
subject to
</DIV>

<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt">67
</DIV>

<DIV style="font-family: 'Times New Roman',Times,serif">
<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">certain sales caps. The Company pays Takeda a royalty on incremental net sales of the
Company&#146;s <I>Niaspan </I>and <I>Advicor </I>products in the United States above a certain baseline amount. This
co-promotion arrangement has a three-year term commencing January&nbsp;2004 and provides for residual
payments to Takeda after the three years, if the parties do not renew the agreement. Residual
payments that may be due to Takeda after December&nbsp;31, 2006, are not included in the table above.
The Company will pay the CSO a royalty based on net sales of the Company&#146;s <I>Niaspan </I>and <I>Advicor</I>
products during a five-year period beginning January&nbsp;1, 2002 (the &#147;CSO Agreement&#148;). The
royalty amounts payable to the CSO are subject to a cumulative minimum of $50&nbsp;million over the term
of the CSO Agreement, and are not to exceed $65&nbsp;million over such contract term. Through
December&nbsp;31, 2005, the Company had incurred $56.2&nbsp;million in royalties related to the CSO
Agreement.
</DIV>

continued success of the Company&#146;s relationship with its co-promotion, commercialization, and

Arisaph, Barr, Duramed, Biovail, and Jerini; the ability to respond to competitive pressures from
competing therapies for the treatment of cardiovascular, metabolic, respiratory and other
conditions that are the focus of the Company&#146;s products; the ability to successfully develop and

expectations regarding the FDA&#146;s approval of a labeling change for our <I>Niaspan </I>products, the
ability to continue the Company&#146;s strong financial performance and that of its products; the
ability to increase its stock price; the ability to protect the strength of its patents; the
ability to commercialize its products outside the United States; the ability to make continued
investments in research and development with the anticipated amount of such investments; the
ability to achieve the Company&#146;s goals for future sales levels, operating margins, earnings and
profit growth, and shareholder value; expectations regarding the impact of certain non-recurring

and <I>Teveten HCT </I>within the medical community, the ability of the Company to generate increasing
sales of <I>Advicor </I>without diminishing the sales of <I>Niaspan</I>, the success of the Company&#146;s
relationship with Takeda, Merck KGaA, Oryx, Barr, Duramed, Biovail and Jerini, the Company&#146;s
ability to avoid the re-importation of the Company&#146;s products into the United States at prices that
are lower than those maintained by the Company in the United States, the Company&#146;s ability to
attract and retain sales professionals and increase the size of its sales force, the Company&#146;s
ability to successfully develop (both internally and through sponsored research arrangements with

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B>ITEM 7A. QUANTITATIVE AND QUALITATIVE DISCLOSURES ABOUT MARKET RISK.</B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The Company&#146;s exposure to market risk is limited primarily to fluctuating interest rates
associated with variable rate indebtedness that is subject to interest rate changes in the United
States. The Company does not use, nor has it historically used, derivative financial instruments to
manage or reduce market risk.
</DIV>

<DIV style="font-family: 'Times New Roman',Times,serif">






<DIV align="center" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 18pt"><B>MANAGEMENT&#146;S ANNUAL REPORT ON INTERNAL CONTROL OVER<BR>
FINANCIAL REPORTING</B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Our management is responsible for establishing and maintaining adequate internal control over
financial reporting for the Company. Our internal control over financial reporting is a process
designed by, or under the supervision of, our Chief Executive Officer and Chief Financial Officer,
and effected by our Board of Directors, management, and other personnel to provide reasonable
assurance regarding the reliability of financial reporting and the preparation of financial
statements for external purposes in accordance with generally accepted accounting principles. Our
internal control over financial reporting includes those policies and procedures that:
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;(1)&nbsp;pertain to the maintenance of records that in reasonable detail accurately and fairly
reflect our transactions and dispositions of our assets;
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;(2)&nbsp;provide reasonable assurance that transactions are recorded as necessary to permit
preparation of financial statements in accordance with generally accepted accounting principles,
and that our receipts and expenditures are being made only in accordance with authorizations of our
management and board of directors; and
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;(3)&nbsp;provide reasonable assurance regarding prevention or timely detection of unauthorized
acquisition, use or disposition of our assets that could have a material effect on the financial
statements.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Internal control over financial reporting cannot provide absolute assurance of achieving
financial reporting objectives because of its inherent limitations. Internal control over
financial reporting is a process that involves human diligence and compliance and is subject to
lapses in judgment and breakdowns resulting from human failures. Internal control over financial
reporting also can be circumvented by collusion or improper management override. Because of such
limitations, there is a risk that material misstatements may not be prevented or detected on a
timely basis by internal control over financial reporting. Also, projections of any evaluation of
effectiveness to future periods are subject to the risk that controls may become inadequate because
of changes in conditions, or that the degree of compliance with the policies or procedures may
deteriorate. However, these inherent limitations are known features of the financial reporting
process. Therefore, it is possible to design into the process safeguards to reduce, though not
eliminate, this risk.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Management uses the framework in Internal Control-Integrated Framework issued by the Committee
of Sponsoring Organizations of the Treadway Commission, or COSO, to evaluate the effectiveness of
our internal control over financial reporting. Management assessed our internal control over
financial reporting using the COSO framework as of the end of our fiscal year. Based on our
evaluation under the framework in Internal Control &#151; Integrated Framework, we believe our internal
control over financial reporting as of December&nbsp;31, 2005 was effective.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Ernst &#038; Young LLP, an independent registered public accounting firm that audited our financial
statements included in this Annual Report on Form 10-K, has issued an attestation report on
management&#146;s assessment of our internal control over financial reporting. Such attestation, which
expresses unqualified opinions on management&#146;s assessment and on the effectiveness of our internal
control over financial reporting as of December&nbsp;31, 2005, is included in this Item&nbsp;8 under the
heading &#147;Report of Independent Registered Public Accounting Firm.&#148;
</DIV>

<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt">72
</DIV>

<!-- PAGEBREAK -->
<P><HR noshade><P>
<H5 align="left" style="page-break-before:always"><A HREF="#tocpage">Table of Contents</A></H5><P>

<DIV style="font-family: 'Times New Roman',Times,serif">

<DIV align="center" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 18pt"><B>REPORT OF INDEPENDENT REGISTERED PUBLIC ACCOUNTING FIRM</B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt">The Board of Directors and Shareholders of Kos Pharmaceuticals, Inc.
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;We have audited management&#146;s assessment, included in the accompanying Management&#146;s Annual
Report on Internal Control Over Financial Reporting, that Kos Pharmaceuticals, Inc. maintained
effective internal control over financial reporting as of December&nbsp;31, 2005, based on criteria
established in Internal Control&#151;Integrated Framework issued by the Committee of Sponsoring
Organizations of the Treadway Commission (the COSO criteria). Kos Pharmaceuticals, Inc.&#146;s
management is responsible for maintaining effective internal control over financial reporting and
for its assessment of the effectiveness of internal control over financial reporting. Our
responsibility is to express an opinion on management&#146;s assessment and an opinion on the
effectiveness of the company&#146;s internal control over financial reporting based on our audit.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;We conducted our audit in accordance with the standards of the Public Company Accounting
Oversight Board (United States). Those standards require that we plan and perform the audit to
obtain reasonable assurance about whether effective internal control over financial reporting was
maintained in all material respects. Our audit included obtaining an understanding of internal
control over financial reporting, evaluating management&#146;s assessment, testing and evaluating the
design and operating effectiveness of internal control, and performing such other procedures as we
considered necessary in the circumstances. We believe that our audit provides a reasonable basis
for our opinion.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;A company&#146;s internal control over financial reporting is a process designed to provide
reasonable assurance regarding the reliability of financial reporting and the preparation of
financial statements for external purposes in accordance with generally accepted accounting
principles. A company&#146;s internal control over financial reporting includes those policies and
procedures that (1)&nbsp;pertain to the maintenance of records that, in reasonable detail, accurately
and fairly reflect the transactions and dispositions of the assets of the company; (2)&nbsp;provide
reasonable assurance that transactions are recorded as necessary to permit preparation of financial
statements in accordance with generally accepted accounting principles, and that receipts and
expenditures of the company are being made only in accordance with authorizations of management and
directors of the company; and (3)&nbsp;provide reasonable assurance regarding prevention or timely
detection of unauthorized acquisition, use, or disposition of the company&#146;s assets that could have
a material effect on the financial statements.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Because of its inherent limitations, internal control over financial reporting may not prevent
or detect misstatements. Also, projections of any evaluation of effectiveness to future periods
are subject to the risk that controls may become inadequate because of changes in conditions, or
that the degree of compliance with the policies or procedures may deteriorate.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;In our opinion, management&#146;s assessment that Kos Pharmaceuticals, Inc. maintained effective
internal control over financial reporting as of December&nbsp;31, 2005, is fairly stated, in all
material respects, based on the COSO criteria. Also, in our opinion, Kos Pharmaceuticals, Inc.
maintained, in all material respects, effective internal control over financial reporting as of
December&nbsp;31, 2005, based on the COSO criteria<B>.</B>
</DIV>

<DIV align="center" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 18pt"><B>KOS PHARMACEUTICALS, INC. AND SUBSIDIARIES<BR>
NOTES TO CONSOLIDATED FINANCIAL STATEMENTS</B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 12pt"><B>1. General</B>
</DIV>


<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Kos Pharmaceuticals, Inc. (&#147;Kos&#148; or the &#147;Company&#148;) develops and commercializes prescription
pharmaceutical products principally for the cardiovascular, metabolic and respiratory markets.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;On July&nbsp;28, 1997, the Company received clearance from the United States Food and Drug
Administration (&#147;FDA&#148;) to market <I>Niaspan<SUP style="font-size: 85%; vertical-align: text-top">&#174;</SUP> </I>for the treatment of mixed lipid disorders, a condition
in which a patient is observed to have several abnormalities in the levels of the fatlike
substances, called lipids, that contribute to heart disease. <I>Niaspan </I>is the only once-a-day
prescription and the first extended-release formulation of any type of product with niacin as the
active ingredient ever approved by the FDA for the treatment of mixed lipid disorders. <I>Niaspan </I>is
indicated for the following: (i)&nbsp;reduce elevated total cholesterol, low-density lipoprotein
cholesterol, commonly referred to as LDL or &#147;bad cholesterol,&#148; and apolipoprotein B, another lipid
particle, and increase low high-density lipoprotein cholesterol, commonly referred to as HDL or
&#147;good cholesterol;&#148; (ii)&nbsp;reduce very high serum triglycerides, which are fatty substances in the
blood that contribute to heart disease; (iii)&nbsp;reduce elevated total and LDL cholesterol when used
in combination with a bile-binding resin, which is a different class of drugs that reduces bad
cholesterol; (iv)&nbsp;reduce recurrent nonfatal myocardial infarction, or the recurrence of nonfatal
heart attacks; and (v)&nbsp;promote the regression or slow the progression of atherosclerosis, which is
a medical condition involving the narrowing of the arteries to the heart, when combined with
bile-binding resins. Additionally, <I>Niaspan&#146;</I>s prescribing information references its ability to
significantly reduce lipoprotein (a), which is referred to as the &#147;very bad cholesterol&#148; and is an
independent risk factor for coronary heart disease.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;On January&nbsp;28, 2002, the Company launched <I>Advicor<SUP style="font-size: 85%; vertical-align: text-top">&#174;</SUP></I>, a solid-dose drug containing <I>Niaspan </I>and
lovastatin, which is a currently marketed cholesterol-lowering drug, for the treatment of mixed
lipid disorders. The Company believes that a once-a-night tablet with the combined complementary
properties of its <I>Niaspan </I>product and lovastatin represents an effective method for treating
patients with mixed lipid disorders.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;On March&nbsp;8, 2004, the Company announced that it had entered into a product acquisition
agreement with Aventis Pharmaceuticals Holdings Inc. (the &#147;Azmacort Acquisition Agreement&#148;) and a
finished product supply agreement (the &#147;Azmacort Supply Agreement&#148; and together with the Azmacort
Acquisition Agreement, the &#147;Aventis Agreements&#148;) with Aventis Pharmaceuticals Inc. (collectively
with Aventis Pharmaceuticals Holdings Inc., &#147;Aventis&#148;) to acquire global rights to the <I>Azmacort</I><SUP style="font-size: 85%; vertical-align: text-top">&#174;</SUP>
(triamcinolone acetonide) inhalation aerosol franchise. The transaction was completed on March&nbsp;31,
2004. Accordingly, Kos began recording revenue for all sales related to the <I>Azmacort </I>product
beginning April&nbsp;1, 2004.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;On April&nbsp;12, 2005, the Company entered into a co-promotion agreement (the &#147;Duramed
Co-promotion Agreement&#148;), a licensing and manufacturing agreement (the &#147;Barr License and
Manufacturing Agreement&#148;), and a settlement and license agreement (the &#147;Barr Settlement and License
Agreement&#148;, and collectively, the &#147;Barr Agreements&#148;) with Barr Laboratories, Inc. and certain of
its affiliates (&#147;Barr&#148;), contemporaneous to the resolution of the patent litigation involving the
Company&#146;s <I>Niaspan </I>products. Under the Barr License and Manufacturing Agreement, Barr agreed to
stand ready to supply Kos Life Sciences, Inc. (&#147;KLS&#148;), a wholly-owned subsidiary of Kos, certain
quantities of <I>Niaspan </I>and <I>Advicor </I>tablets, under or pursuant to Barr&#146;s Abbreviated New Drug
Applications (&#147;ANDAs&#148;), as and when approved, and granted KLS a license to use, market and sell, at
a future date, Barr&#146;s versions of the Company&#146;s <I>Niaspan </I>and <I>Advicor </I>products as and when approved
in the United States. Barr received an initial license fee and receives quarterly payments to
remain compliant with FDA current Good
</DIV>

<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt">80
</DIV>

<!-- PAGEBREAK -->
<P><HR noshade><P>
<H5 align="left" style="page-break-before:always"><A HREF="#tocpage">Table of Contents</A></H5><P>

<DIV style="font-family: 'Times New Roman',Times,serif">



<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">Manufacturing Practices (&#147;cGMP&#148;) and to stand ready to meet the Company&#146;s manufacturing
requirements on short notice.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The Barr Settlement and License Agreement sets forth the terms of the settlement of the
litigation between Kos and Barr. It permits Barr to launch a generic version of <I>Niaspan </I>and
<I>Advicor</I>, as well as future dosage formulations, strengths or modified versions of the products,
under terms of an exclusive license commencing on September&nbsp;20, 2013, approximately four years
earlier than the last-to-expire KLS patent. Pursuant to the Duramed Co-promotion Agreement,
Duramed Pharmaceuticals, Inc. (&#147;Duramed&#148;), an affiliate of Barr, promotes the Company&#146;s <I>Niaspan </I>and
<I>Advicor </I>products to physicians who mostly specialize in women&#146;s healthcare. In consideration of
Duramed&#146;s performance of its co-promotion obligations, the Company pays Duramed royalties subject
to certain sales caps.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;On May&nbsp;2, 2005, KLS entered into a strategic commercialization and research and development
alliance with Biovail Corporation and certain of its affiliates (&#147;Biovail&#148;) in the area of
cardiovascular disease (the &#147;Biovail Agreements&#148;). Pursuant to the Biovail Agreements, KLS acquired
the rights to the <I>Teveten<SUP style="font-size: 85%; vertical-align: text-top">&#174;</SUP> </I>(eprosartan mesylate) and <I>Teveten HCT </I>(eprosartan
mesylate/hydrochlorothiazide) products (collectively, the &#147;<I>Teveten </I>Products&#148;), and obtained
exclusive sales and marketing rights to the <I>Cardizem<SUP style="font-size: 85%; vertical-align: text-top">&#174;</SUP> LA </I>(diltiazem hydrochloride) product in the
United States. The Company began recording revenue for sales related to the <I>Teveten </I>Products and
<I>Cardizem LA </I>in May&nbsp;2005.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;On July&nbsp;7, 2005, the Company received notice from Biovail that Andrx Corporation had filed
with the FDA an ANDA that would, if approved, allow Andrx Corporation to market a generic version
of the <I>Cardizem LA </I>product. On August&nbsp;10, 2005, a lawsuit alleging patent infringement against
Andrx Pharmaceuticals, L.L.C. and Andrx Corporation (collectively &#147;Andrx&#148;) in Biovail&#146;s name was
commenced in the United States District Court for the District of Delaware (&#147;Delaware District
Court&#148;). Upon receiving a second Paragraph&nbsp;IV certification from Andrx Pharmaceuticals, L.L.C.
directed to additional <I>Cardizem LA </I>tablet strengths of 120, 180, 240, 300, and 360 mg added by
amendment to Andrx&#146;s ANDA, on October&nbsp;14, 2005, a second complaint alleging additional patent
infringement was filed in Biovail&#146;s name in the Delaware District Court. These two civil actions
have been consolidated by the Court for all purposes. Under the current case schedule, fact and
expert discovery is scheduled to close on September&nbsp;5, 2006, and the trial is scheduled for April
9, 2007. These dates, however, may change.
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;On November&nbsp;6, 2005, KLS entered into an exclusive Collaboration and License Agreement (the
&#147;Jerini Agreement&#148;) with Jerini US, Inc. (&#147;Jerini&#148;), a subsidiary of Jerini AG, for the
development, marketing and distribution of Jerini&#146;s compound, Icatibant, a peptidomimetic
bradykinin B2 receptor antagonist, in the United States and Canada. In connection with the Jerini
Agreement, KLS paid a $14.2&nbsp;million upfront licensing payment (the &#147;Jerini Licensing Payment&#148;) to
Jerini. Additionally, Kos consummated an investment in Jerini AG (the &#147;Jerini Investment&#148;).
</DIV>

<DIV align="left" style="font-size: 10pt; margin-top: 6pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;No assurance can be given that the Company&#146;s products can be successfully marketed, that
products under development can be successfully formulated or manufactured at acceptable cost and
with appropriate quality, or that required regulatory approvals will be obtained. The Company is
subject to a number of other risks including, but not limited to, the continued market acceptance
of the Company&#146;s products, the expected continued growth in sales of the Company&#146;s products, the
success of the Company&#146;s relationship with its co-promotion, commercialization and strategic
alliance partners, the Company&#146;s ability to successfully develop (both internally and through
sponsored research arrangements with third parties) and commercialize new products under
development and within expected timeframes, fluctuating buying patterns by the Company&#146;s
wholesalers and distributors, the accuracy of the inventory data supplied by wholesalers and
distributors pursuant to IMAs, the adequacy of the Company&#146;s reserves for income taxes, the
Company&#146;s ability to maintain coverage of its products by government agencies and the effects of
the loss of such coverage with such agencies, such as the Centers for Medicare and
</DIV>
